# Kunooz al-Hikmah: The Stored Treasures of Wisdom for the Masters of Infallibility

## Introduction to the Book

These chapters are a series of sessions delivered aloud, walking a listening audience through a single book: *Kunooz al-Hikmah al-madhkurah li-arbab al-ismah*, The Stored Treasures of Wisdom for the Masters of Infallibility. The book itself is a set of answers, written by a man who answered reluctantly, to questions put to him; the sessions take those answers in order and open them one by one.

The speaker begins with the author and the one book he left, then with the opening praise of the book, the three human natures, and God, who is not known as things are known. Blessings follow upon the chain — the Imams, the gates, the duat (دُعَاة) of Najran (نَجْرَان) — and then the account of how the questions came to be written at all. From there the series moves through what was there before origination, the fifteen ranks of the repentant, the veils and the registers of rank, the Black Stone and the cycles of return, the lowlands beneath the Kabah, Harun and Musa, the sacrifice at Mina and the standing at 'Arafat, the making of a dai, the Mahdi and the Qaim (قَائِم), and at last the rolling up of the sky and cycles without end.

The book was written by Sayyidna (سَيِّدُنَا) Ali ibn Hibatullah, known as al-Shamsi. His father, Sayyidna Hibatullah (سَيِّدُنَا هِبَةُ اللهِ) ibn Ibrahim (إِبْرَاهِيم), is called Sayyidna Hibatullah as well, and the reader should keep the two apart.

## 1. The Author and His Only Book

The book's name is *Kunooz al-Hikmah al-madhkurah li-arbab al-ismah* — The Stored Treasures of Wisdom for the Masters of Infallibility. These are treasures of wisdom preserved for the *arbab al-ismah* (أَرْبَابُ الْعِصْمَةِ), the masters of infallibility. The *arbab al-ismah* are those who possess *'ismah* (عِصْمَة), infallibility. In one sense the phrase points to the Ahl al-Bayt (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) and their progeny, but that is not the meaning intended here. The Ahl al-Bayt and the *Aimmah Tahirin* (أَئِمَّة طَاهِرِين), the Pure Imams, are the masters of *kulli ismah* (كُلِّيِّ عِصْمَة), total infallibility — it is impossible for them to err, and they are free from all sin. The *duat* (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), by contrast, are the masters of *juzi ismah* (جُزْئِي عِصْمَة), partial infallibility. This distinction was clarified in a *risalah* (رِسَالَةٌ) by Sayyidna Ali ibn Hussain al-Jamari, who explained that the *Aimmah Tahirin* are the masters of *kulli ismah*, while the *duat*, and especially the *qaimi duat* (قَائِمِي دُعَاةٍ), the standing *duat*, are the masters of *juzi ismah*. These matters have been stored for the *duat* — the *arbab al-ismah* of *juzi ismah* — so that they may draw new understanding from them.

The book was written by Sayyidna Ali ibn Hibatullah, known as al-Shamsi, a figure of great station. Sayyidna Hibatullah Sahib — that is, Sayyidna Hibatullah ibn Ibrahim — had several children, and he performed *nass* (نَصٌّ), designation, upon his eldest son. That eldest child was Sayyidna Ali ibn 'Ibad, whose full lineage runs: Sayyidna Ali ibn Hibatullah ibn Ibrahim ibn Muhammad, the *shajarah*, or family tree, continuing without interruption all the way back to Adam (عَلَيْهِ السَّلَامُ). Sayyidna Ali ibn Ibad, however, passed away during his father's lifetime, and so he became a *ghayr-munfarid Dai* (غَيْرُ مُنْفَرِد دَاعٍ), a non-independent *Dai* (دَاعِي). Sayyidna Hibatullah Sahib then performed *nass* upon another son, who became eighth in the order of succession — the rank Sayyidna Ali ibn Ibad had held at his death. Sayyidna Ali ibn Ibad had himself performed *nass* upon yet another son, Sayyidna Ismail (إِسْمَاعِيل), who was tenth in the order of succession. It is said that Sayyidna Ismail ibn Hibatullah possessed the most excellent outward form — *surat* (صُورَة) — in the community, *jamaat* (جَمَاعَة), of the *wulat* (وُلَاة). Another child of Sayyidna Hibatullah Sahib was Sayyidna Hasan ibn Ibad, who likewise became a *Dai* of great station. Yet another was Sayyidna Jafar (جَعْفَر), who was the seventy-fourth *Dai*. He did not reach the rank of *Dai* in succession, yet his station is great, and he composed *qasaid*, odes, among other works.

The book *al-Kawakib al-Durriyah*, itself a work of great station, opens with a *qasidah* (قَصِيدَة) by Sayyidna Jafar ibn Hibatullah.

As for Sayyidna Ali ibn Hibatullah, he wrote only one *risalah* — only this single book, nothing besides. He wrote it during his father's lifetime. His station among the *duat* is nonetheless exceedingly high.

*Bismillah al-Rahman al-Rahim*. In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful. Above the *Bismillah* is written the *Ism al-Azam* (الِاسْمُ الْأَعْظَم), the Greatest Name. In the *risalah* by Sayyidna al-Wajih — that is, 'Abd al-Ali (عَلِي) ibn Hasan al-Wajih — great wonders and secrets are set down, and the entire *risalah* addresses a single question: whether the *Ism al-Azam* is greater, or whether the *nur*, the light, of the *Bismillah* is greater. After a lengthy discussion, he concluded that the *nur* of the *Ism al-Azam* is more excellent than that of *Bismillah al-Rahman al-Rahim*. That is why the *Ism al-Azam* is written above the *Bismillah*.

The script in which it appears is *khatt al-Teseri*, the Teseri script. In this script the first dot is a *khatam* (خَاتَمٌ) — a seal, that is, a ring — and this ring alludes to the *Ahl al-Sharif* (أَهْلُ الشَّرَف), the noble family. The three marks above it carry their own significance. There are also verses, *ashar*, by Amir al-Muminin on the *Ism al-Azam*. The *madd* (مَدٌّ) — the elongation mark above it — refers to the *muawanah*, the assistance, received from the *Hudud 'Aliyah* (حُدُودٌ عَالِيَة), the higher ranks of the hierarchy; the *madd* points toward that. The precise details are not at hand at present, but God willing, should the occasion arise, they will be brought forward. That is a matter separate from the present text.

The book at hand is *Kunooz al-Hikmah al-madhkura li-arbab al-ismah*, The Treasures of Wisdom Mentioned for the People of *'Ismah*. That is the name of the book, and it is the work of Sayyidna Ali ibn Hibatullah al-Mukarrami.

*Audhu billahi minash shaytanir rajim.* (I seek refuge in Allah from the accursed Satan.) *Bismillah al-Rahman al-Rahim.* (In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful.) *Wa bihi nastain, sallallahu 'ala rasulihi Sayyidina Muhammadin wa ala aali Sayyidina Muhammadin wa barik wa sallim.* (And in Him we seek help. May God's blessings, peace, and benedictions be upon His messenger, our master Muhammad, and upon the family of our master Muhammad.)

The name of the book is *Kunooz al-Hikmah al-madhkurah li-arbab al-ismah*, The Stored Treasures of Wisdom for the Masters of Infallibility — treasures of wisdom preserved for the *arbab al-ismah*, the masters of infallibility. The *arbab al-ismah* are those who possess *'ismah*, infallibility. In one sense the phrase points to the Ahl al-Bayt and their progeny, yet that is not the meaning intended here. The Ahl al-Bayt and the *Aimmah Tahirin*, the Pure Imams, are the masters of *kulli ismah*, total infallibility — it is impossible for them to err, and they are free of all sin. The *duat*, by contrast, are the masters of *juzi ismah*, partial infallibility. This distinction was clarified in a *risalah* by Sayyidna Ali ibn Hussain al-Jamari, who explained that the *Aimmah Tahirin* are the masters of *kulli ismah*, while the *duat*, and especially the *qaimi duat*, the standing *duat*, are the masters of *juzi ismah*. It is for these *arbab al-ismah* — the masters of *juzi ismah*, who are the *duat* — that these matters have been stored, so that they may draw new understanding from them.

The book was written by Sayyidna Ali ibn Hibatullah, known as al-Shamsi, a figure of great station. Sayyidna Hibatullah Sahib — that is, Sayyidna Hibatullah ibn Ibrahim — had several children, and he performed *nass*, designation, upon his eldest son. That eldest child was Sayyidna Ali ibn 'Ibad, whose lineage runs: Sayyidna Ali ibn Hibatullah ibn Ibrahim ibn Muhammad, the *shajarah*, the family tree, continuing without interruption all the way back to Adam. Sayyidna Ali ibn Ibad, however, passed away during his father's lifetime, and so he became a *ghayr-munfarid Dai*, a non-independent *Dai*. Sayyidna Hibatullah Sahib then performed *nass* upon another son, who became eighth in the order of succession — the rank Sayyidna Ali ibn Ibad had held at his death. Sayyidna Ali ibn Ibad had himself performed *nass* upon yet another son, Sayyidna Ismail, who was tenth in the order of succession. It is said that Sayyidna Ismail ibn Hibatullah possessed the most excellent outward form — *surat* — in the community, *jamaat*, of the *wulat*. Another child of Sayyidna Hibatullah Sahib was Sayyidna Hasan ibn Ibad, who likewise became a *Dai* of great station. Yet another was Sayyidna Jafar, the seventy-fourth *Dai*. He did not reach the rank of *Dai* in succession, yet his station is great, and he composed *qasaid*, odes, among other works. The book *al-Kawakib al-Durriyah*, itself a work of great station, opens with a *qasidah* by Sayyidna Jafar ibn Hibatullah.

As for Sayyidna Ali ibn Hibatullah, he wrote only one *risalah* — only this single book, nothing besides. He wrote it during his father's lifetime. His station among the *duat* is nonetheless exceedingly high.

*Wal-hamdulillahi rabbil-alamin.* (And all praise is for Allah, Lord of the worlds.)

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> *A note on terms.* When this chapter speaks of the *Hudud 'Aliyah*, the higher ranks, the word
> repays a look at its root. *Hudud* is the plural of *hadd*, the boundary or limit that marks
> where one thing ends and another begins; the root h-d-d means to set an edge — whether a
> physical border or a sharpened blade — and from it also comes *hadid*, iron, the hard and
> cutting metal. In the wider usage of this tradition the same word names the fixed limits God has
> set, and by extension the defined penalties for crossing them. Read this way, the ranks of the
> hierarchy are not merely levels but thresholds: each is a defined edge, and the assistance the
> chapter says flows down from the higher ranks passes across precisely such ordered boundaries.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 2. Praise, the Three Human Natures, and the Unknowable God

*Bismillah al-Rahman al-Rahim. Al-hamdu lillahi rabbil-alamin. Wal-aqibatu lil-muttaqin. Was-salatu was-salamu 'ala Sayyidina Muhammadin wa alihi ajmain.*

> In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful. All praise is for Allah, Lord of the worlds. And the best outcome is for the righteous. And blessings and peace be upon our master Muhammad and all his family.

The book opens with praise:

*Al-hamdu lillahil-azim jalla nasutun khas min riyadh (رِيَاض) ahlil-ikhtisas.*

> All praise is for Allah, the Great, who manifested a special *nasut* from the gardens of the people of specialty.

All praise and laudation are for Allah, that Exalted Being who created the special *nasut* (نَاسُوت) from the *riyadh* — the gardens — of the people of specialty, the *ahl al-ikhtisas* (أَهْلُ الِاخْتِصَاص). The varieties of *nasut* are several:

1. *Nasut adna* (نَاسُوتٌ أَدْنَى) — the lowest *nasut*, also known as *nasut tabii* (نَاسُوتٌ طَبِيعِيٌّ) (the natural *nasut*) or *nasut tanasuli* (نَاسُوتٌ تَنَاسُلِي) (the procreative *nasut*)
2. *Nasut awsat* (نَاسُوتٌ أَوْسَطُ) — the middle *nasut*, also known as *nasut dhilli* (نَاسُوتٌ ظِلِّيٌّ) (the shadow *nasut*)
3. *Nasut khas* — the special *nasut*

The *ahl al-ikhtisas* are the *hudud al-dawah* (حُدُودُ الدَّعْوَة) — the ranks of the *dawah* (دَعْوَة) — in the *jazair* (جَزَائِر), the regions, together with the *muminin*, the believers, and the *hadarat* (حُضَرات), the dignitaries, among them. The *nasut khas* (نَاسُوت خَاصّ) is fashioned from them.

*Wa jaala al-nasut al-dani min riyadh al-madunun fil-maqam.*

> And He made the lower *nasut* from the gardens of those who are lesser in rank.

The *nasut dani*, the lower *nasut*, is made from the *riyadh* of those who are more distant, lesser in rank. This refers to the *nasut dhilli* or the *nasut tabii*.

*Ahmaduhu hamda man abda al-mana al-zahir.*

> I praise Him with the praise of one who has manifested the apparent meaning.

The praise offered is the worship of one whose meaning is luminous.

*Wa 'arafa haqqa al-ism al-zahir.*

> And he knew the reality of the apparent name.

He knew the reality of this apparent name — that is, the author has grasped both the outward, *zahiri* (ظَاهِرِي), reality of the name and its inner, *batini* (بَاطِنِيّ), meaning.

*Wa ashhadu an la ilaha illa alladhi tasawa fil-ajzi 'an idraki huwiyyatihi al-mutaaliyah awwalu mawjudin wa akhiruhu min bariyyatihi.*

> And I bear witness that there is no god but He, in the inability to comprehend whose exalted essence the first of existing things and the last of His creation are equal.

The witness borne is that there is no deity worthy of worship but He, and that the first existent thing and the last existent thing in His creation are equal in their inability to comprehend His exalted essence. The first existent thing is the First Intellect, *'aql-e-awwal*. The last existent thing, from among His creation, is the believer — the believer who has entered the *dairah-e-iman*, the circle of faith. Both are equal in their inability to attain gnosis of God. The First Intellect has not attained greater gnosis of God, nor has the last existent thing attained a lesser gnosis than the First Intellect. Such gnosis is simply not possible for either.

The word "thing," *shay* (شَيْء), carries weight here. A "thing" is that which possesses real existence, as in *inna Allaha 'ala kulli (كُلِّيٌّ) shayin qadir* — Indeed, Allah has power over all things. Here "thing" refers to the believer, whose value and existence are real. *La shay'* — "no-thing" — by contrast, is that which is a mirage: it appears to be water, but on reaching it, nothing is there. It has no real value. This usage appears in three places in the Holy Quran:

- *Inna Allaha 'ala kulli shayin qadir* — Allah has power over all things — meaning especially over every believer
- *Inna Allaha bikulli shayin alim* — Indeed, Allah is knowledgeable of all things
- *Inna Allaha bikulli shayin muhit* — Indeed, Allah encompasses all things

In their inner meaning, all three refer to the believer, and ultimately to the Imam of the time.

This equality in inability — shared by the First Intellect and the last existent thing — is reflected in what is said about Sayyidna Ali ibn Sulayman (سُلَيْمَان). When believers would come to meet him, he would rise to receive them. Similar conduct has been observed among the *duat*, who would remain standing while exchanging the handshake of *juma*, Friday, and on other occasions. When asked why he would not be seated, he replied: "I remain standing because I stand to shake a believer's hand, for he is my companion in the *Ahl al-Sharif*." In the *tawzi* (تَوْزِيع), the distribution, of the *Ahl al-Sharif*, the believer takes his share just as the Imam takes his share. The believer is his companion in the *Ahl al-Sharif*, and so he honors him by rising.

The same principle underlies what Sayyidna Hussain ibn Ismail, may his holy secret be sanctified, wrote about Sayyidna Abdullah (عَبْدُ اللّٰه) ibn Muhammad and Sayyidna Ahmad al-Jamari. He used the expression *afdal al-mawjudat* (أَفْضَل الْمَوْجُودَات) — the most excellent of existing things — in reference to Sayyidna Abdullah ibn Muhammad. Those present began to wonder at the *Dai*'s words. But *afdal al-mawjudat* here means he who is present in the *dawah* — that is, among the believers — and among them, he is the most excellent. That was the point being made.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> A note for the reader: two terms this chapter leans on carry more in their roots than
> translation shows. The wider tradition explains that the root of *batin*, b-t-n, names the belly
> and so the inner, hidden part of anything — al-batin is thus the concealed inner meaning lying
> beneath a text's outward wording, which is precisely what is being drawn up when the "reality of
> the apparent name" is set beside its inner sense. Likewise the root of *aql*, ع-ق-ل, first means
> to tie down — the same word names the rope that tethers a camel — and from that comes the mind
> that binds and holds meaning fast; a related teaching preserved in this tradition describes it
> as a restraining, unifying faculty far deeper than ordinary reasoning. Keeping this in view
> helps clarify what is meant by the First Intellect.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 3. The Prophet, His Brother, and the Veiled Successors

*Wa ashhadu anna Muhammadan 'abduhu wa rasuluh.*

And I bear witness that Muhammad is His slave and His messenger.

The witness continues: *al-murtaqi min nasut al-khas ila jawar dhi al-izzah wal-jabarut* — the one who ascended from the special *nasut* to the proximity of the Possessor of Might and Power. In the *Miraj*, the Ascension, the Prophet traveled through the special *nasut*. That *nasut* was fashioned from the *riyadh* of the *ahl al-ikhtisas* — the people of specialty: the *hudud* (حُدُود) of the *dawah*, the believers, the *hadarat* and the *hudud* of the regions. From those *riyadh* the *nasut khas* was made, and through it the Prophet ascended and was elevated to the proximity of the Possessor of Might and Power. *Jabarut* (جَبَرُوت) derives from *jabr* and denotes the all-powerful, the compellingly mighty.

*Sallallahu 'alayhi wa ala akhihi* — may God's blessings be upon him and upon his brother, *al-mustakhlaf ala al-ummah al-Muhammadiyyah talbisan li-sur al-radiyyah*, the appointed successor over the Muhammadan community, clothing himself in lowly forms. Before the Prophet's passing, at Ghadir Khumm (خُمّ), he declared:

> *Man kuntu mawlahu fa-hadha 'Aliyyun mawlahu.*

Of whomsoever I am master, this Ali is his master.

From that moment, the Prophet lived the remainder of his life within the *hijab* (حِجَابٌ), the veil, of Ali, and Ali lived within the *hijab* of the Prophet. This is what the text alludes to. Sayyidna Ismail ibn Ibrahim is reported to have stated — though without full elaboration — that the *awsiya* (أَوْصِيَاء), the successors, pass away before the *natiq* (نَاطِقٌ), the speaker-prophet.

The remaining life of Mawla Ali was, in essence, the life of the Prophet. He was within the Prophet's *hijab*. As declared:

> *Lahmi lahmuka wa dami damuka.*

My flesh is your flesh and my blood is your blood.

This proceeds from *ruh wahidah*, a single soul. When the Prophet's body was present, Ali was within his *hijab*; and when the realities were made manifest, Ali was the one in whom they appeared.

In the Battle of Jamal, Mawla Ali said to Lady Aisha words to the effect of divorce. The Prophet had granted Ali this right after his passing: "I give Ali the right that after my passing, he may divorce you." The proof of Ali's status as the Prophet's very self comes from the Holy Quran:

> *Taalaw abnaana wa abnaakum wa nisaana wa nisaakum wa anfusana wa anfusakum.*

Come, let us call our sons and your sons, our women and your women, our selves and your selves.

When "our sons" was called, Hasan and Hussain were brought. When *anfusana* (أَنْفُسَنَا) — "our selves" — was called, Ali was brought. He thus became the *nafs* (نَفْس), the self, of the Prophet.

*Al-muhtajib bihi al-ashir (الْعَاشِر) bil-amr al-nahir* — the Tenth who is veiled by him in the manifest affair.

The one who occupied the rank of *al-ashir*, the Tenth, became veiled, and that rank passed onward. The first *al-ashir* did not remain in that station; he changed into another rank. The one who then became *al-ashir* is the one who, in the subsequent series — within its sphere — occupied that station: the lofty intellect within its sphere. What is intended here is drawn from the *sahib al-qawr* (صَاحِبُ الْقَوْر), the master of the cycle: the *juz-e-idafiyyah*, the relative part, of that cycle. It has been consistently noted that *al-ashir* in this passage does not refer to *al-ashir* in the absolute sense, but rather to the *juz-e-idafiyyah* of this *qawr* — the *Dai* who is the relative portion of this cycle, not the *sahib al-qawr* himself. He is the *juz-e-idafiyyah*, and he became veiled by the Prophet.

*Wa 'ala fatin (فَاتِن) mudawij al-aimmah* — and upon the youth who is the spouse of the Imams.

The *fatin*, the youth, is Lady Fatimah, who became the spouse of the Imams. She was the wife of Ali, and from Ali she became the mother and, in the deeper sense, the companion of the Imams. *Bi-sirrin daqqa manahu* — with a secret whose meaning is most profound. As the Prophet declared in the presence of the Commander of the Faithful: *khalifatu abiha* — she is her father's successor — and *hujjatu baliha* — she is the *hujjah* (حُجَّة) of her husband. The most excellent *hujjah*, *afdal al-hujjah* (أَفْضَلُ الْحُجَّةِ), is she herself. It is for this reason that Shahr Rajab al-Murajjab — which is attributed to Ali (ع) — has its first night as a night of great station, and that first night alludes to Lady Fatimah, who is the *hujjat al-uzma* (حُجَّةُ الْعُظْمَى), the greatest *hujjah*.

The *duat* of that era did indeed possess knowledge of these matters. As for the caliphs, some things were known to them. Abu Bakr and Umar had some knowledge of these realities, having lived in proximity to the time of *'Abd al-Muttalib*, but their knowledge was not complete. There is a well-known incident in which Umar said to Hasan or Hussain, in substance, that the honor upon your head was not placed there by me, but by you and your father. This is also preserved in broader tradition.

On another occasion, Imam Hasan or Hussain seated himself upon the *minbar*, the pulpit, and declared: "This *minbar* belongs to my father." And Umar himself testified: "Yes, this *minbar* belongs to your father," and then took his seat below.

The successor appointed for the community of the Prophet was Lady Fatimah al-Zahra. It is for this reason that her passing occurred within six months. When she would arrive, the Prophet would stand. *Wa ajlasaha fi maqamiha* — and he would seat her in his place, *fi maqamihi*, in his own place. This was a great sign: an indication that the one who would sit in his place is Lady Fatimah al-Zahra.

*Wa ghaba 'an hadha al-sirr illa ahluhu wa ahwah* — and this secret was hidden from all but its people and its folk. Those who were unaware of this secret — of its hidden matters — remained unaware of it, except for those who were its people.

*Wa 'ala al-Hasan al-mustawda' sirr al-imamah* — and upon al-Hasan, the trustee of the secret of the Imamate. Hasan is the trustee of the secret of the Imamate.

*Wal-qaim min al-nasut ila al-lahut ka-arbab al-zaamah* — and the one who rises from *nasut* to *lahut* (لَاهُوت) like the masters of leadership. He rises from *nasut* to *lahut*, just as the *arbab al-zaamah* (أَرْبَابُ الزَّعَامَة) — the masters of leadership, the noble ones — rise likewise. Even while designated as the trustee, *mustawda* (مُسْتَوْدَع), he possesses both a *nasut* and a *lahut*. That is the meaning.

All the *duat* are connected to Hasan, and the *Aimmah* (أَئِمَّة) are connected to Sayyidna Hussain. But all the *duat* are connected to Imam Hasan. In the Holy Quran, Surah al-Nas alludes to Sayyidna Hasan:

> *Qul audhu birabbin-nas.*

Say, "I seek refuge in the Lord of mankind."

The word *nas* here refers to those who are *manusina bid-dawah* — intimate with the *dawah*, who have affection for it. The Lord of mankind is Sayyidna Hasan. And *rabb al-falaq*, the Lord of the dawn:

> *Qul audhu birabbil-falaq.*

Say, "I seek refuge in the Lord of the dawn."

The word *falaq* (فَلَقٌ) means to split, and from that splitting, the Imamate began.

*Wa 'ala al-Hussain al-muzhar al-ghaybah* — and upon al-Hussain, the one who made manifest the occultation, *ghaybah* (غَيْبَة). He made his own self absent. *Bil-qatli lada ahl al-raybah* — through death at the hands of the people of doubt. Those who harbored doubt watched as his murder took place. An Imam cannot be killed, for he is infallible. Yet he is the one who made death manifest; he manifested his own death.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> *A note for the reader.* Several words this chapter leans on carry root-meanings worth naming.
> *Hijab* comes from a root meaning "to veil or bar": it is whatever screens one thing from
> another — a curtain or partition preventing sight across it — so to say one lived "within the
> *hijab*" of the other is to speak of concealment, not distance. *Nafs* derives from a root
> meaning "to breathe"; from breath comes the breathing self, which in spiritual writing also
> names the lower self the seeker must master. *Natiq* is from a root meaning to speak and
> articulate (it also underlies *mantiq*, logic): the speaker who voices the outward law, standing
> opposite the silent one who keeps its inner sense. *Hujja* is from a root meaning to set out
> purposefully — the same root as *hajj* — hence the proof one betakes oneself to, or the person
> embodying it. The Ghadir declaration is preserved widely in the tradition.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 4. Blessings on the Chain: Imams, Gates, and the Duat of Najran

*Wa 'alal aimmati min dhurriyyatihi majami' al-anwar* — and upon the Imams from his progeny, the assemblages of lights. The phrase *majami al-anwar* (مَجَامِعُ الْأَنْوَارِ) carries a precise sense. *Anwar* (أَنْوَارٌ) (lights) and *asrar* (أَسْرَار) (secrets) are two different things. *Asrar* is used for *niyyat* (intention). Concerning *anwar*: Sayyidna Sulayman has written in this very book that he is the *jami al-asrar* — the assembler of secrets — of the *duat* of Yemen who became *duat* after Sayyidna Dhuayb bin Musa. There the word used is *asrar*, not *anwar*. And *anwar* refers to the *suwarat* (صُورات), the luminous forms, of which he is the assembler.

*Wa asma al-ayn al-azimah fil-ali min al-asrar* — and the names of the great essence in the high secrets. These *Aimmah Tahirin* are the names of the great essence, dwelling in the great and high secrets. The great essence, and all the *Aimmah* who come until the Day of Resurrection, are veiled by him; he is their *hijab* (veil). These Imams are a *hijab*, and their veiled one is Amir al-Muminin.

*Wa 'ala Imam al-zaman al-mutawari bil-hijabi* — and upon the Imam of the Time, who is concealed by the veil. *Al-mustatir bil-sirr an jami' al-baraa* — the one who is hidden by the secret from all of creation. The original self remains hidden, and what has appeared is a *hijab*.

*Wa sallama 'alayhim wa ala abwabihim al-mutalali' anwaruhum fis-suwar* — and may (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) and upon their *abwab* (gates), whose lights shine forth in the forms.

*Wa 'ala hujajihim wa ala duat hadha al-dawr al-maymun al-qain khamsina alfa sanah mimma tauddun* — and upon their *hujjahs*, and upon the *duat* of this auspicious *dawr* (دَوْرٌ) (era), which will last for fifty thousand years of what you count.

The blessing falls upon the *duat* of this *dawr* — the *dawr* of the Qaim, may God's (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ). This *dawr* will last for fifty thousand years. The number has its source in this: when the Qaim comes, fifty thousand years begin after him. The present era is therefore included within it. The *dawr al-satr* (دَوْرُ السَّتْر) (era of concealment) ended with Sayyidna Sulayman Sahib; the seven eras were finished — as confirmed by calculation as well — and this *dawr* is included in the *dawr* of the Qaim. Hence the words *al-qain khamsina alfa sanah mimma tauddun*.

*Wa Sulayman bin Hasan al-mujtama ladayhi asraru duat al-Yaman* — and Sulayman bin Hasan, with whom are gathered the secrets of the *duat* of Yemen. The secrets were gathered in his keeping alone — a station of great distinction.

*Wa Jafar al-sabi fis-sirr al-mamun* — and Jafar, the seventh in the protected secret. Jafar is the seventh: being the twenty-eighth, he became the seventh in the manifest, protected secret.

*Wa mimun min lala* — and a Mim (مِيم) from above. Mim refers to Muhammad bin Fuad (مُحَمَّد بِن فُؤَاد), that intermediate figure who came in between, non-independent though he was.

*Al-qaim bi-fard al-jihad fi malain* — the one who established the duty of jihad among the assembly. He is the one who establishes the duty of jihad in the midst of the assembly — that is, among the people. His jihad was tremendous. Had the position of Sayyidna Muhammad bin Fuad been ruined — as happened in the case of Abd al-Muttalib and others — the *dawah* itself would have stood in peril, and its history would have changed entirely. Yet the jihad he carried out was tremendous.

*Wa al-ayn al-mutafajjir 'ala lisanihi yanabi' al-asrar* — and 'Ayn (عَيْن), upon whose tongue gushed forth the springs of secrets. Ayn means Sayyidna Ali bin Sulayman (سَيِّدُنَا عَلِيُّ بْنُ سُلَيْمَانَ), from whose tongue the springs of secrets gushed forth. The various secrets of the doctrines — their springs and fountains — were made manifest through his tongue. In his *haqaiq* (حَقَائِق) and his other books he presented sublime matters of doctrine, things that had not been presented before. Of *Shual al-Anwar*, which is the first book, he himself wrote that this *Shual al-Anwar* is in the *haqaiq* (realities) and the *daqaiq* (دَقَائِق) (subtleties): *hadha al-kitab fil haqaiq wad-daqaiq*. From there the books of subtleties began.

Sayyidna Jafar did not write a single book on *haqaiq*. All his books are on *hujjat* (حُجَّة) (proof) or *tawil* (تَأْوِيل) (interpretation), although his own station is very lofty. Mim and 'Ayn are often mentioned to refer to the *muhtajib* (مُحْتَجِب), but here they are used to refer to the *duat*.

*Al-ati bi-ma la yakhtur 'alal afkar* — bringing forth that which had never occurred to the thoughts. He brought forth things that had never crossed people's minds and had never before been made manifest. The books that stand in his name — *Anwar al-Shashaniyyah* and others — and the lofty realities within them, are very special things that he presented.

*Wa saribuhu alladhi ghada ghidha al-nufus bi-asrar al-malakut al-quddus* — and his drinking companion, who became the nourishment of souls with the secrets of the holy *malakut* (مَلَكُوت). His *sarib*, his drinking companion, means Sayyidna Ibrahim. He became the nourishment of souls with the secrets of the holy *malakut* — from the secrets of Allah, the Holy.

*Wa Muhammaduhu al-muhajir min baldatihi li-uluwwi shanih wa rutbatihi* — and his Muhammad, the emigrant from his town for the loftiness of his station and his rank. Muhammad here means Muhammad bin Ismail (مُحَمَّد بِن إِسْمَاعِيل). He is the first *Dai* who emigrated from his town, to Najran. Until then the *dawah* had been in Yemen: in Sanaa, in Haraz, and in Safan. He is the first *Dai* who left Yemen and came to Najran, for the loftiness of his station and rank. He is also the first and only *Dai* who was given permission to lead prayers in *ghayr jazirah* (غَيْرِ جَزِيرَة) — outside the Arabian peninsula — and so he led prayers in Habasha.

After him, the *duat* did not simply return to Yemen. A few remained where they were, and then they too moved. It was in the time of Sayyidna Hasan Shihab and Sayyidna Ali bin 'Ibad that they were there, and in the time of Sayyidna Ghulam Hussain Sahib they came fully to Najran — though in fact this was somewhat earlier.

The emigration was toward Najran, and there was also the going to Abuna, to Sayyidna Muhammad bin Ismail. Sayyidna Sulayman Sahib, in his time, toured Abuna and stayed there for quite some time. The journey from Yemen to India was one of great hardship, taking one year and passing through various cities of Syria and elsewhere. There was a stay in Abuna as well, and a mosque was built there which still exists today. Sayyidna Hussain bin Hasan later enlarged that mosque, and in the time of Sayyidna Hussain bin Ismail it was further expanded and rooms were built for those sitting in *itikaf* (اعْتِكَاف) (seclusion). It is a very magnificent mosque.

Abuna is not a port. It is a mountainous area, very far from the coast — about two hundred kilometers from Najran, remote and mountainous. Sayyidna Sahib went there, and it has been visited in later times as well. The grave of Sayyidna Rashid, Rashid bin 'Isa, is also there, and the grave was visited and the *ziyarat* (زِيَارَة) performed. Sayyidna Rashid is the one who, after becoming a *Dai*, stood for six years in his prayer niche making up his missed prayers. It is possible that in the early period of the *dawah* some prayers were missed, and he made up six years of them standing in the prayer niche.

After the passing of Sayyidna Hussain bin Hasan, Sayyidna Hussain bin Ismail gave one of their companions — Ahmad by name, a boy from Yemen, very intelligent and an exceptionally good young man — the prayers of Sayyidna Hussain bin Hasan: four years of prayers that had been missed, for in those last days he had become like an angel and could not keep track of his prayers. So four years of prayers were made up. And when Sayyidna Hussain bin Hasan passed away, the walk to his side was made barefoot from the house — a considerable distance, of the order of the distance from there to Grand Road station — and it was covered barefoot.

*Sallam 'alayhim ajmain abad al-abadin* — forever and ever. May peace be upon all of them, for all time.

*Bismillah al-Rahman al-Rahim.*

*Fatiha.*

*Bismillah al-Rahman al-Rahim...*

*Fatiha.*

*Bismillah al-Rahman al-Rahim...*

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> *An editorial aside:* Readers meeting the language of gathered *asrar* and the *haqaiq* of
> *Shual al-Anwar* for the first time may find help in a related teaching preserved in this
> tradition, which speaks of the tree in Eden in its good sense as pointing to "the knowledge of
> realities, which is forbidden to those who are not worthy of it" — a knowledge belonging to the
> Imams, whose attainment "gives rise to eternity and brings release from this perishing bodily
> world." That framing explains why such secrets are described as gathered in a single trusted
> keeper rather than broadcast: restriction here is a matter of worthiness, not of withholding.
> The same wider tradition connects the theme of concealment to the very beginning of the human
> cycle, describing Adam, peace be upon him, as the one who began the cycle of concealment — a
> useful backdrop to the vocabulary of veil and hidden secret used throughout this chapter.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 5. Before the Words Begin: Refuge, the Call to Prayer, and the Prayer for the Dai

*Bismillah al-Rahman al-Rahim.* By His name, the Exalted, the High. *Wa bihi nastain, wa sallallahu 'ala rasulih, Sayyidna Muhammadin wa ala aali Muhammadin wa barik wa sallim.*

The opening rests on the statement of Allah Taala:

> *Wa idha qarata al-Quran fastaidh billahi min al-shaytan al-rajim.*

And when you recite the Quran, seek refuge in Allah from Satan the accursed.

A question stands at the threshold. This is the Book of Allah itself, so why was the command to seek refuge — the *taawwudh* (تَعَوُّذ) — given? Why should the reciter of the Quran take shelter before beginning? The Book is the word of Allah and the source of guidance, yet people have been led astray by this very thing. A *dai* once held up this *Kitabullah* — the Book of Allah — and declared that it is this very book that has led communities astray: those who misinterpreted it and drew wrong meanings from it drove whole communities into error. For this reason, by the command of Allah, *taawwudh* is adopted at the opening of the *dawah* — both when the Quran is recited and when other *duas* containing Quranic verses are read.

The adhan is the exception. *Taawwudh* is not said while giving the adhan; only *Bismillah* is said there. Within the adhan, moreover, the two final utterances of *La ilaha illallah* are not alike. The very last one is not recited in the same emphatic manner as the first; it is spoken in a soft tone, and a difference between the two must be maintained. That closing utterance is bound to the *ihtizaz* (اهْتِزَاز) of a particular being — a *dhat* (ذَاتٌ) for whom special prayers exist — in that he is the last *mushir*, the last person who will depart from his entire circle in the blink of an eye. That matter will happen in the blink of an eye, and so the utterance is not to be elongated.

Concerning the verse *wa qulil hamdu lillah* — "and say, 'All praise is for Allah'" — the *salawat* is recited after it. Since the verse mentions only the saying of the *takbira* (تَكْبِيرَة), it can seem as though one should move directly to "Allahu Akbar," without *Bismillah*, as if no other words may stand between the words of the adhan and the words of the verse.

That is not the case. After *wa kabbirhu takbira* — "and magnify Him with great magnification" — the *salawat* may be recited first, and then *wa qulil hamdu lillah... wa kabbirhu takbira*: "Allahu Akbar, Allahu Akbar, Allahu Akbar." There is no hard rule. *Bismillah* should be said before the adhan, and there is no strict requirement that the adhan begin immediately after *wa kabbirhu takbira*. Whatever is read from one's own side before it does no harm.

On the question of order — the *tartib* — Imam-ul-Mamool explained in a *tashrih* that *hamd* (حَمْد), praise, should come first, and that is why the verse says *qulil hamd*. The *salawat* is recited afterward and so belongs later. *Hamd* stands higher: it is recited first, with nothing placed before it, and the *salawat* follows. This is how he explained the order.

One further point attaches to this verse. It is the last verse of Surah Bani Israil, and among the names that verses carry, it is called *Ayat al-Izz* (آيَةُ الْعِزّ) — the Verse of Honor.

### The Prayer for the Dai

*Bismillah al-Rahman al-Rahim.*

> *Allahumma wahfaz dhata al-dai al-jamia li-asrarihim al-mujtamaa ladayhi anwaruhum.*

O Allah, protect the being of the *dai*, who is the collector of their secrets and with whom their lights are gathered.

This speaks of the future. In life, *asrar* means *riyah* (رِيَاح), and *anwar* means *surah* (صُورَة) — forms. During the *dai*'s lifetime, the *riyah* of the *hudud al-dawah* or of the *muminin*, and the *anwar* in the sense of *surah*, are not yet fully formed. The *riyah* emerge after death: after burial or after passing away, they begin to emerge, for three days. That is a matter for later. But later they will be gathered with him — their *asrar*, meaning their *riyah*, and their *anwar*, meaning their *surah*.

*Wahmi hawzatahu* — and protect his *hawzah* (حَوْزَة). A *hawzah* is a place around which a boundary has been drawn, a walled enclosure. Here it means the center of the *dawah*. Protect the center of the *dawah* *min fawaran firawni al-zaman* — from the aggression of the Pharaoh of the age — for *li-kulli dain firawn*: for every *dai* there is a Pharaoh, an opponent of the *dawah*.

The matter was raised in Hujjat that during the time of Sayyidna Husayn ibn Ahmad there appeared to be no such Pharaoh openly opposing the *dai*. It was a magnificent era, a time of complete peace; the disturbances came later. The answer given was that the *dai* of the Dawoodis is also a kind of Pharaoh — in a passive sense. He did not openly confront, but had he found the opportunity, he would have done as Dawood ibn Qutub did and made a claim, becoming a *muddai* (مُدَّعِي). In the present case, however, the opposition is greater. There it was not so; here there is a Pharaoh.

*wa ballighhu jamia mamoolihi ya Rahim ya Rahman* — and may Allah convey to him all his aspirations and desires, fulfilling the *dai*'s wishes. *Ya Rahim ya Rahman* is itself a *tasbih* (تَسْبِيح) — the *tasbih* of the Pir.

A question of order arises here as well: should it not be *ya Rahman ya Rahim*? In the Quran, *Rahman* always precedes *Rahim*. In books, however, this order is not always observed, because of rhyme schemes — *qafiyah-paymai*. Since *Firawn al-zaman* appeared before it, *ya Rahim ya Rahman* follows for the sake of the rhyme. In the divine attributes generally, *Rahim* always comes second: *Rauf-ur-Rahim*, *Aziz-ur-Rahim*. It has never come first in the Quran. In the *duas* and *sahifas* (صَحِيفَات), however, *Rahim* precedes *Rahman* in several places. *Rahman* has also appeared alone in the Quran, as in the verse:

> *Qulidullaha awidur-Rahman.*

Say, "Call upon Allah or call upon the Most Merciful."

No other name of Allah has appeared alone in this way.

In Surah al-Hajj, at one place, the text reads *Ya Dhal-Jalali wal-Ikram* (يَا ذَا الْجَلَالِ وَالْإِكْرَام) — O Possessor of Majesty and Honor. The verses of Surah al-Hajj from 58 to 65 are greatly blessed. Among them are eight verses called *Mafatih al-Jinan* (مَفَاتِيحُ الْجِنَان) — the Keys to the Gardens — corresponding to the eight heavens, whose keys these verses are. There is great virtue in reciting them. One verse alone contains fifteen of the *Asma al-Husna*, the Beautiful Names of Allah, and *Ya Dhal-Jalali wal-Ikram* is counted among them. *Rahman* appears there as well.

The prayer continues. *wa anir masabih dawatihi* — and illuminate the lamps of his *dawah*, meaning the *hudud al-dawah*; illuminate the entire *dawah*. *wa kaththir 'adada shiatihi* — and increase the number of his followers. *wa asri ilayna mawaddahu* — and cause to flow toward us his substance, his *mawad* (مَوَادّ), and his confirmations, his *tayidat* (تَأْيِيدَات). *wa allimna bi-lataifi barakatihi* — and teach us through the subtleties of his blessings, directing their subtle dimensions toward us. *warzuqna tahannunahu wa shafaqatahu* — and grant us the *dai*s kindness, his *tahannun*, and his compassion, his *shafaqah*. *wa afwahu wa rahmatuhu* — and his forgiveness, his *afw*, and his mercy, his *rahmah*, that he may forgive our sins.

*Ya 'Aliyyu ya Azim, ya Aliyyu ya Azim.* This too is a *tasbih* — one performed on Wednesdays.

## 6. The Reluctant Answerer: How These Questions Came to Be Written

The treatise opens with an account of its own occasion. Its first words are: *Wa badu, fa-inna-ha waradat masail 'aliyatun* — "And after this, there came lofty issues." Certain lofty questions had been presented *ila al-hadrat al-samiyah* — to the elevated presence — that is, to the venerable *dai*, *min ba'd al-awlad*, from some of the children, the *awlad*. That presence is Sayyidna Hibtullah al-Muayyad, and the questions are those which the *abna' al-dawah*, the children of the *dawah*, had brought forward. *Fa-sata al-amru al-alami* (الْعَالَمِي): the epithet *al-alami* belongs to Sayyidna Hibtullah, one of whose titles is *Alamuddin* (عَلَمُ الدِّين) — the banner of religion, the high banner of the faith. The command of *al-alami*, of the *dai*, "flowed *ila 'abdihi akhar*" — to his other servant. Hamza b. al-Walid is the speaker. Sayyidna Ali records that the command came upon him, *al-haqir*, *bi-an yujibu ala sualatihi* — that he should answer those questions — *wa ma lahu bi-aqlamihi tatr* — "and with his pens, to set it down in writing as well." The answer was to be not merely spoken but committed to writing.

Sayyidna Ali had initially wished to decline. *Wa kuntu aradtu an atadhira ila mawlayya li-qusuri 'ilmi wa budi fitrati wa fahmi* — "And I had wanted to make an excuse to my master on account of the deficiency of my knowledge and the distance of my nature and understanding." His knowledge was insufficient, his understanding weak, his capacity far short of what the task required. *Fa-aradtu mawania ba'd al-bilad* — "And I presented the obstacles posed by distant lands." Questions had arrived from people across many lands; how could all of them be answered? That was the excuse. Yet *fa-asratu al-taata* — "in the end, I hastened to obey." He submitted and accepted the command. *Wa asdaqtu al-murad* — "And I found the desired object" — he attained the goal he had been set. *Wa tadharratu ila suratihi al-nuraniyah* — "And I supplicated through his luminous form," his *surah nuraniyah* (صُورَةٌ نُورَانِيَّة). *Wa awwaltu ila mawaddihi al-qudsaniyah* — "And I relied upon his holy substance," his *mawad qudsaniyah* (مَوَادٌّ قُدْسَانِيَّة).

Here a remarkable statement appears. Sayyidna Ali says, "I supplicated through your luminous form" — yet the *dai*'s form, his *surah*, has not yet been constituted, for the *dai* is still *natiqah* (نَاطِقَة), *hissiyah* (حِسِّيَّة), and *namiyah* (نَامِيَة). The *surah* is formed only after passing away. Yet the *dai*s station is such, and his person so magnificent, that his very *natiqah* must be understood as the blessed *surah* — the one that will be fully realized as a *surah* on the Day of Resurrection.

*Wa wassamtu hadhihi al-risalah bi-kunuz al-hikmah* — "And I named this treatise *Kunooz al-Hikmah*" — the Treasures of Wisdom. These are treasures stored for the People of *Ismah*, the *arbab al-ismah*. In this context, *arbab al-ismah* does not refer to the Imams but to those who hold partial *ismah* — *juzwi 'ismah* (جُزْئِيَّةُ عِصْمَةٍ) — that is, the *duat*. The treasures have been stored for them, so that they may draw substance, *mawad*, from them. The introduction to this *risalah* had already established a teaching that no *dai* before had articulated: that the *aql al-awwal* (الْعَقْلُ الْأَوَّل) — the first intellect — and the *akhir al-mawjud* (آخِرُ الْمَوْجُودِ) — the last existent being, meaning the *mustajib* (مُسْتَجِيب) who has just entered the *hadrat sharifah* (حَضْرَةٌ شَرِيفَة) and stepped into the circle of faith — are equal. Equal in what? In the *marifah* (مَعْرِفَة), the gnosis, of the *Mubda' Taala*, the Exalted Originator. The *aql al-awwal*, for all its proximity, holds no advantage in the *marifah* of the *Mubda Taala* (مُبْدَأٌ تَعَالَى); it stands perplexed and bewildered in precisely the same way every believer does. No *dai* had said this before.

*Fa-ma kana fiha min sawab* (صَوَاب) — "And whatever is correct in it" — whatever correctness the book contains — *fa-mansubun ilayya ila man ajra lisani* — "then it is attributed to the one who caused my tongue to flow," the one who gave his tongue the power to express and record the matter. The credit belongs to him, to the *dai*. The word *sawab* — from *sad-waw-alif-ba* — means correctness, not reward. The common phrase "Jazakallah bil-khairi was-sawab" is often read as though *sawab* were *thawab* (ثَوَاب), meaning reward. Here, however, *sawab* means correctness: "What you have said is correct." The two words are easily confused, and the distinction is easily missed.

*Ila man ajra lisani* — to the one who caused his tongue to flow. *Wa ma kana fiha min khata, wa billahi al-iyadh* — "And whatever mistake is in it — and in Allah I seek refuge — " *fa-mansubun ilayya* — "then it is attributed to me." The error is his own. *Idh ana alaqtu al-jani* — "As I am a sinner" — a *jani* (جَانِي). The word *jani* is sometimes used as a term of endearment for a beloved or a child, but in Arabic it means sinner.

*Wa ana akhidhu hadh al-ahd al-muakkad* — "And I am taking this confirmed covenant" — this *'ahd* (عَهْد). *Wa mithaquhu al-mughalladh al-mushaddad* — "And its pact, which is severe and strong." *Ala man waqaa hadha fi yadayhi* — "Upon whosoever this falls into his hands" — upon anyone whom this book reaches and who becomes aware of its secrets — *an la yaqraa fihi* — "that he should not read in it" — *wa la yataammala maanihi* — "and should not ponder its meanings" — *illa bi-fushin min huwa al-naqid* — "except with the permission of one who is the *naqid* (نَاقِد)." A *naqid* is one who can tell the good from the bad, who knows what is right and what is wrong, and who criticizes with insight. *Wal-basir* — "and the insightful one" — one who possesses inner sight. *Man lahu al-taqdim wal-takhir* — "the one who has the authority of *taqdim* (تَقْدِيم) and *takhir* (تَأْخِير)" — who can advance or withdraw a person within the *hudud*, and who holds full authority over such appointments. Only with his permission may the book be read. *Wa illa, fa-huwa bariun min al-Aliyy al-mabud* — "Otherwise, he is free of the Exalted, the Worshipped" — meaning Allah, the Most High, Whom we worship, will be the judge of that person's case. *Wa min kaffat al-mahdudin wal-hudud* — "And from all the *mahdudin* and *hudud*" — from their obedience, he is released.

*Wa hadha qawl al-sail hujjatuhu* — "And this is the statement of the questioner, his argument." The one writing this introduction has set down the entire question within it. *Awradtuhu bi-fassih ruyatihi* — "I have presented it with the clarity of his vision" — in the very manner in which the question was brought to the *dai*. *Hafidhallahu al-maqam al-azim* — "May Allah protect the great station," the *maqam al-azim*. *Wal-alam al-rabbani al-alim* — "And the lordly, knowing banner" — one who is a lordly scholar, deeply learned: *Alamuddin Hibtullah b. Ibrahim al-Muqrami*. *Hafidhahullahu wa dafaa 'anhu al-aswa'* — "May Allah protect him and ward off evils from him."

The word *aswa* raises a question: does it imply that the *dai* has evils, and that the prayer is for their removal? Sayyidna al-Sijilmasi said, "My sins are like mountains, *idhama intalat al-jibali*" — they have risen so high. Such language is not how personal failings are ordinarily described. The meaning of these expressions lies elsewhere. The *dai*'s sins, in this understanding, are the good deeds intended but left incomplete — writing a book, recording a noble thought, benefiting someone — thwarted by some obstacle before they could be carried through. The intention was there, but it could not be translated into action. This they count as a grave sin.

The same question arises regarding the Quranic verse in which God says to the Prophet that his former and latter sins are forgiven. God forbid that the Prophet should have sins. On this point, Sayyidna Husayn b. Ismail, Qaddas Allahu Ruhahu, recorded an account. He recounted that he and his brother were studying when Muhsin came — the brother of Sayyidna Ali b. Husayn — a thin and frail man who was not himself a great scholar but was always in the presence of the *dai*, his brother, and used to ask many questions. He was both Muhsin's son-in-law and his father-in-law: Muhsin b. Ali. He came and asked, "May I put a question?" The question concerned the opening verse of Surah al-Fath: *Inna fatahna laka fathan mubina* — "Indeed, We have granted you a clear victory" — *li-yaghfira laka Allahu ma taqaddama min dhanbika wa ma taakhkhara* — "so that Allah may forgive you your former and latter sins." What were the Prophet's former and latter sins?

Sayyidna looked and asked, "Do you know?"

The answer, drawing from the *dai*'s substance, his *mawad*, is this: the verse does not refer to the Prophet's own being, which is pure, *bari*. Rather, the *hujab* (حُجَّاب) — those who rise from the *hudud al-dawah* and later become deputies — do not initially possess the substance of *ismah*; *ismah* comes to them later. In the interval, they are unable to fully govern themselves and sometimes err. The allusion is to them. The former and latter sins refer to the Prophet's *hujab* — those among them who leaned toward Abu Bakr. That inclination was the first sin; the second was yielding a portion of Ali's *wilayah* (وِلَايَة) to him, so that the system of the *dawah* might be preserved. Allah was displeased, and a verse came: *Wa in ashrakta layahbatanna amaluka* — "And if you associate partners, your work will surely become worthless." The address is to the very Prophet from whom the lesson of faith was received. It does not refer to outward shirk. Its meaning is: if even an iota of anyone else is included in the *dawah* of Ali, in the *wilayah*, the work will be nullified. And here Allah declares that all his matters have been completely forgiven.

*Wa hadha qawl al-sail hujjatuhu awradtuhu bi-fassihi wa hayatihi*. *Wa dafaa 'anhu al-aswa'*. The *aswa* — the evils — are in reality the *mahdudin* and the *hudud al-dawah*. They are what is truly meant by the evils.

*Wa ballighhu kulla ma yarumu wa yahwa* — "And convey to him everything that he intends and desires" — praying that Allah may fulfill whatever the master intends and desires. *Wa 'alayhi min abdihi afdhal al-salam* — "And upon him from his servant, the best of peace." *Wal-matlub min tafaddulihi jawab ma saaltu anhu* — "And what is requested from his grace is the answer to what I have asked about." These are the words of the questioner: he requests that, from the master's grace, an answer be given to what he has asked. *Bi-hadhb al-manni alayya wal-tafadduli wal-tatawwuli* — "By the favor of grace upon me, and grace and bounty" — the grace that the master would bestow. *Wa uqaddimu bayna yadayhi ma asalu anhu ilayhi* — "And I present before him what I ask about to him" — concerning the *wilayah* of the *hudud* of the religion. *Wa al-dallina alayhi* — "And those who guide toward him." *Wa abrau mimman ankara addanihum* — "And I absolve myself from those who deny their lowest ones" — meaning the *hudud al-din* (حُدُودُ الدِّين). *Wa atadharrau ilayhi bi-wusuli alam (عَالَمٌ) al-ibda' al-kiram* — "And I supplicate to him through the connection of the noble world of creation" — the honorable ones. *Wa bi-haqqi kulli wasiyyin 'azimin wa imamin* — "And by the right of every great successor and Imam" — every *wasi* (وَصِيّ) and Imam. *Wa bi-kulli dairatin tadawwarat* — "And by every circle that revolved" — the various circles of Imamate and of *qaimiyat* (قَائِمِيَّة). *Wa nuktatin sharifatin takawwanat* — "And a noble point that came into being."

*Tahjibuni* — that the master may not set him aside, given the weakness he acknowledges. *Fa-mathaluka man yasturu 'ala misli* — "For your example is one who covers the faults of someone like me." *Wa idha tafaddalta an la tukhayyibni mimma ammaltu* — "And when you show grace, that you may not disappoint me in what I have hoped for." *Bi-haqqika al-azim la tahmalu haqqi* — "By your great right, do not neglect my right" — do not withhold an answer. *Wa an tarhamani birrika wa ammika* — your piety, *bir*, and your beneficence, *am* — your *sirrika*, your forgiveness, *afw*, your help, *awn*, and your kindness, *ghawth* — in the two worlds, the *darayn* (الدَّارَيْن): this world and the hereafter. *Wal-nazar ila ma afuh bihi nafsan aw tafawwuhan* — and what will be said, what the soul is filled with or has already uttered. *La yujidu lahu mana* — "which has no meaning" — for among the questions, some will touch matters in which the soul is implicated or which it has expressed. For those, *fa-albisni sawb al-sitr wal-ghina'* — "then clothe me with the garment of concealment and contentment" — the garment of *sitr* (سِتر) and *ghina*: place the veils. *Wa umnun 'alayya bi-tatabui ihsanika* — "And favor me with the continuity of your benevolence." *Razaqani Allahu ridaka* — "May Allah grant me the sustenance of your pleasure" — your *rida*. *Wa qad lakam audhu billahi wa bil-ali* — "And indeed, I seek refuge in Allah and in the Exalted" — *an la yadhunna al-dhannu anni ma asalu illa mutaddian fasaahat lisan wa ghadharat jinan* — "that the one who thinks may not think that I am asking only as a claimant of eloquence of tongue or sharpness of mind." That, the questioner insists, is not his motivation. *Wa innama qasartu an al-shurbi min ra's (رَأْسٌ) al-main* — "But rather, I fell short of drinking from the head of the spring" — from the *dai* himself, from benefiting directly. *Wa qad alima al-hatm al-azim al-barr al-rahim bi-aqdari abdihi* — and the great, pious, merciful decree knew what the questioner had stated — *kaifa wa sadqa min sadaqatihi* — that whatever is bestowed will be a charity, a *sadaqah* (صَدَقَة), from his charities. *Wa nafkhatan min nafahatihi* — "And a breath from his breaths." *Wa la akhira qurbanan illa ma qaddamtu min masturihi* — "And I do not seek any other nearness except what I have presented from his written work." That is all.

This is the introduction to the treatise. The questions have not yet begun. The questioner did not frame his questions with great precision, pointing things out directly; the framing is not of that kind.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> A note for the reader: two terms carry the weight of this opening. The first is *marifa*, from
> the root ع-ر-ف, which in the wider tradition denotes knowing by recognition — meeting something
> and knowing it firsthand rather than merely knowing about it; hence gnosis of God as the
> knowledge of one who has come to recognize Him, not only reasoned toward Him. This clarifies why
> the first intellect can hold no advantage over the newest believer: recognition of the
> Originator is not a quantity of information to be accumulated. The second is *ahd*, from the
> root ع-ه-د, the language of solemn promise — to bind oneself by a pledge and be charged with
> keeping it. A related teaching preserved in this tradition applies the same word both to the
> compact between God and humanity and to the charge of office Ali lays upon his governor Malik
> al-Ashtar, which is the register in which the closing oath upon every future reader should be
> heard.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 7. What Was There Before Origination

In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful. And in Him we seek help. May Allah's blessings and peace be upon His Messenger, Sayyidna Muhammad, and upon the family of Muhammad.

The book is *Kunooz al-Hikmah al-madhkura li-arbab al-ismah* (The Treasures of Wisdom Mentioned for the People of 'Ismah), a work of Sayyidna Ali ibn Hibatullah al-Mukarrami. What follows is the first question and its answer.

*Al-sual al-awwal* (The first question). In the copy at hand the heading reads *awwalan al-sual al-awwal* (Firstly, the first question).

The questioner said:

"*Inni basartu wa taammaltu fima tafaddaltum bihi min kutub awliya Allah (أَوْلِيَآءَ ٱللَّهِ) wa ba'd al-mufidin, lam (لَامٌ) ajid ma dallani 'ala ma kana qabla huduth alam al-ibda'.*"

"Indeed I have seen and contemplated that which you have graciously provided from the books of the friends of God and from some of the benefactors, and I did not find that which would guide me to what existed before the origination of the World of Origination."

The questioner had studied and reflected on the books of the *awliya Allah* (friends of God) and the benefactors under whom he had studied, yet still found nothing to indicate what had existed before the *'Alam al-Ibda'* (عَالَمُ الْإِبْدَاع) (World of Origination).

He continued: *wa kayfa kana al-hal wa ma al-majal wa al-minwal?* (And what was the state of affairs, and what was the scope and the manner?) Before the *'Alam al-Ibda'*, what matters were occurring, what things were taking place, and what were the conditions there? *Wa mimma hadathat 'uqul (عُقُولٌ) alam al-ibda'?* (And from what did the Intellects of the World of Origination come into being?) What pertained to the Intellects of the *'Alam al-Ibda'*?

He then offered what he had already gathered: *fa-qad raayna fi kutub awliya Allah wa afadna anna al-Imam hijabuhu al-khass min riyad al-hadrah* (رِيَاضُ الْحَضْرَةِ) (For we have seen in the books of the friends of God, and we have benefited from the fact that the Imam, his special veil, is from the gardens of the Presence). The Imam's *hijab al-khass* (حِجَابُ الْخَاصّ) (special veil) is fashioned from the *riyad* (رِيَاض) (gardens) of the *hadarat* (presences).

Al-Walid 'Abdullah Sahib would always say that the weight of such an exchange rests not with the question but with the answer — with what the Dai is responding to. The questioner has asked about the Imam's *hijab al-khass*, and in broad terms there are three types of *hijab*:

- *tabii* (طَبِيعِيّ) (natural)
- *dhilli* (ظِلِّيٌّ) (shadowy)
- *khass* (خَاصٌّ) (special)

Beyond these stands the *dhat-i Imam* (ذَاتُ إِمَام) (essence of the Imam). The questioner further asks how this is reflected in the *tasbih* (prayer beads). In the *tasbih*, the first utterance is *Allahu Akbar* (God is the greatest), pointing toward the *hijab al-adna* (اَلْحِجَابُ الْأَدْنَى) (lowest veil), also called the *hijab al-tabii* (اَلْحِجَابُ الطَّبِيعِيُّ) (natural veil) or *hijab al-tanasuli* (الْحِجَاب التَّنَاسُلِي) (procreative veil). The second portion of thirty-three points to the *hijab al-dhilli* (حِجَابُ الظِّلِّيّ) (shadowy veil). The third portion, of *Subhanallah* (Glory be to God), is the *hijab al-khass* (special veil). At the end comes the Imam himself, the *dhat-i Imam*. Through the *tasbih*, these three states are traversed in sequence.

The questioner has thus posed two matters:

1. What existed before the *'Alam al-Ibda'*.
2. What were the circumstances of the *'uqul* (intellects) of the *Alam al-Ibda'* when they came, and what befell them.

Alongside these he presents his own conception: that the *hijab al-khass* is made from the *riyad al-hadrah* (gardens of the presence).

He went on: *wa hijab al-laiq al-tabii min riyad hujaj al-jazair* (حُجَجُ الْجَزَائِر) (and the fitting natural veil is from the gardens of the Hujaj of the Islands), meaning that the veil which follows and is joined to it — the *tabii* — is made from the *riyad* of the *hujaj al-jazair* (Proofs of the Islands). *Tawi ila ba'd nisa al-hijab al-zawahir 'ala hadhihi al-kayfiyyah wujuduhu* (resorting to some of the women of the outward veil upon this modality of its existence): its existence is in this modality. The one who performs the act of marriage with wives is the *hijab al-tabii* of the Imam. *Wa yadharu bi-khalqihi wa min khalqihi fi khalqihi* (And he appears through his creation, from his creation, and in his creation). That veil appears from creation, from the side of creation, and within creation — since it is made from *riyad*, and the *riyad* are from the *makhluqat* (creatures). Once the veils are formed, they take human form; those veils, those *riyad*, appear as human beings.

He asked further: *wa kayfa kana qabla wujud al-samawat wa al-ard* (And how was it before the existence of the heavens and the earth) — before the skies and the earth existed, what was there? *Wa qad 'alimtu mimma wujudha* (and I have known from what its existence is): the manner in which the heavens and the earth came to exist was already known to him. *Wa min ayyi fudulatin wujud al-awwal al-jussa' fi safil al-ard* (and from what remnants is the existence of the first particle in the low part of the earth). From what remnants did the *awwal jussa* — the first particle of the *karr* (كَرٌّ) — come into existence? It was created from the clod in the earth, and the one that comes at the beginning of the *karr* holds a great station. From what remnants, then, did it come, given that there was nothing at that time? *Fa-tafaddal bi al-jawab* (So graciously provide the answer).

*Al-jawab 'ala dhalika al-sual* (The answer to that question). The Dai, Sayyidna Ali ibn Hibatullah, responds:

*Naqulu bi-awnillah al-kabir al-mutaal* (We say with the help of Allah, the Great, the Exalted). *Al-Kabir al-Mutaal* is also a *tasbih* recited on Thursdays. *Wa minnati waliyy al-asr wa al-zaman, 'alayhi salawat al-rahim al-rahman* (and from the favor of the master of the age and time, upon him be the blessings of the Merciful, the Compassionate). Here *rahim* comes first — a reversal of the customary order. In the Quran, *rahim* has never preceded *rahman*; *rahman* has always come first. In *Bismillah al-Rahman al-Rahim*, among the various interpretations, *al-rahman* in one interpretation points to Mawlana Abd al-Muttalib (مَوْلَانَا عَبْدُ الْمُطَّلِب), and *rahim* refers to Mawlana Abu Talib. Mawlana Abd al-Muttalib was certainly the greater of the two: he is the *jadd al-qaim* (جَدُّ الْقَائِم) (grandfather of the Qaim), the *jadd al-ayn* (جَدُّ الْعَيْن) (grandfather of the ayn), and the *jadd al-ayn al-akhirah* (grandfather of the final ayn) as well. *Wa tayidi mawlay al-alam wa sirrihi al-bahir al-muazzam* (and with the support of my master of the world and his manifest and magnified secret): from this support the answer is given.

*Anna al-jawab 'ala dhalika qad sabaqa min mawla al-malik alam al-iman fi kitabihi al-sharif al-mawsum bi-kanz al-ulum* (the answer to that has already been given by the master, the king, the flag of faith, in his noble book named *Kanz al-Ulum*, The Treasure of Knowledge). The answer is already present in the book of Sayyidna Hibatullah — in *Hibat al-Rahman*, in his noble *Kanz al-Ulum*, a very special book. In *Kanz al-Ulum* there is a passage concerning Bibi Fatima al-Zahra to the effect that her station and greatness are such that even the Aimmah Tahirin (the pure Imams) could not attain her gnosis. For this very reason the later Imams are called *Aimmah Fatimiyyin* (أَئِمَّةٌ فَاطِمِيُّون) (the Fatimid Imams). The Imams up to the twenty-first are called *Aimmah Fatimiyyin* because they stand perplexed and bewildered before the station of Bibi Fatima al-Zahra.

The answer proceeds: *'alayka ya ayyuha al-arshad bi al-jawab al-awwala ala al-awwal bi al-qawl al-ali al-mumajjad, ma fihi kifayah kafiyah wa nima al-shafiyah* (Upon you, O rightly guided one, is the first answer to the first question, with the high and glorified saying, in which there is sufficient sufficiency, and what an excellent cure). The saying is high and great; in it there is sufficiency, and it is a cure.

That saying is: *anna wujud 'alam al-ibda' la min shayin wa-la fi shayin wa-la yajibu al-bahth 'an kayfiyyatihi* (the existence of the World of Origination is not from a thing, and not in a thing, and it is not required to inquire about its modality). The natural assumption would be that there was a pre-existing world within which the intellects were created. It is not so. Mawlana Iddan Sahib (مَوْلَانَا عَدَّانٌ صَاحِبُ) also used to say that the world and the intellects are not two separate things — rather, *alam al-ibda' hum al-uqul* (the World of Origination is the Intellects themselves). The *'Alam al-Ibda'* is precisely those intellects whom Allah Taala created: without anything, and within nothing. It is therefore not obligatory — nor even permissible — to inquire into the modality of the *sudur al-ibda* (صُدُورُ الْإِبْدَاع) (emanation of the origination), into how the *ibda* (إِبْدَاعْ) came into existence. *Wa-la sual idh laysa li al-aql fihi majal* (and no question, since the intellect has no scope in it). Human intellects cannot reach the question of how the *ibda* emanated, how it was created, how it appeared; the matter lies far beyond them. *Wa fi al-ajwibah 'an dhalika dalal wa ishrak* (and in the answers to that is misguidance and polytheism). Whoever attempts an answer — saying it happened in this way or that — falls into misguidance and *shirk* (polytheism). *Wa tahawwutun fi bahr al-halak* (and a plunge into the sea of destruction). Since it is not even known how the *Alam al-Ibda'* came into existence, whoever speaks of it plunges into the sea of destruction. *Naudhu billahi min dhalika wa nasaluhu al-nijah min al-mahalik* (We seek refuge in Allah from that, and we ask Him for salvation from the places of destruction).

*Li-anna dhalika muallal fi-man la tajasar nahwahu al-khawatir* (because that is substantiated for one toward whom thoughts do not dare to venture). That matter is established by evidence of a kind that the *khawatir* (خَوَاطِرٌ) — the passing thoughts of the heart — cannot dare approach: how the *Mubdi Taala* (مُبْدِئٌ تَعَالَى) created the *'Alam al-Ibda'*, and what that modality was. Of its modality there can be no knowledge. *Wa-la tuhitu bihi al-awham wa al-damair* (and estimations and inner thoughts do not encompass it). The *awham* (estimations) and *damair* (inner thoughts) cannot encompass that modality. *Wa yahtaj al-arif li-dhalika 'ala an yakuna maahu surah aw aql (عَقْل) aw wahm qabla huduth al-ibda'* (And the gnostic needs for that knowledge that he should have with him a form, an intellect, or an estimation from before the origination of the ibda). For a gnostic to know what existed before the *ibda*, he would need some form, intellect, or estimation belonging to what came before it. *Aw fikrah bal suduruhu 'an al-huwiyyah al-mutaaliyah, la min shayin* (or a thought; rather its emanation is from the Exalted Identity, not from a thing). This much alone stands as knowledge: that the emanation of the *Alam al-Ibda'* came about through the *huwiyyah al-mutaaliyah* (هُوِيَّةُ الْمُتَعَالِيَة) (Exalted Identity) — that is, the *Mubdi Taala*, by His own means — and not from any pre-existing thing.

*Wa qad awdaha al-duat sababu qillati al-wusul ila marifati dhalika* (And the duat have clarified the reason for the scarcity of access to the gnosis of that). The *duat* have explained why access to its gnosis is all but non-existent. *Dhalika li-ajz al-uqul al-ibdaiyyah* (that is due to the inability of the originated intellects). The *'uqul ibdaiyyah* (عُقُولٌ إِبْدَاعِيَّةٌ) — the First Intellect, the Second Intellect, and those that follow — are themselves at a loss to know how the *Mubdi Taala* brought this about. *Fa-kayfa bi-man dunahum min al-suwar al-daniyah* (So how about those below them from the lowly forms?). Human forms are very lowly beside the intellects of the *Alam al-Ibda'*; that modality cannot be known through them.

The answer then turns to the veils. *Wa amma al-hijab al-ali alladhi huwa al-nasut al-khass al-hawi li al-sharaf wa al-ikhtisas wa al-nasut al-tabii al-dani, fa-khudh haqiqata marifatiha min awwal al-tamhid* (As for the high veil, which is the special human form containing honor and specialty, and the natural, lowly human form, take the reality of its gnosis from the beginning of the preface). The *hijab al-ali* (حِجَابُ الْعَالِي) (high veil) is the *nasut al-khass* (نَاسُوت الْخَاصّ) (special human form), which holds honor and specialty; the *nasut al-tabii* (نَاسُوت الطَّبِيعِيّ) (natural human form) is the lowest *nasut*. The reality of both is to be taken from the preface at the very opening of this book, where it is written: "All praise is for Allah, who made the *nasut al-khass* from the *riyadat ahl al-ikhtisas* (رِيَاضَةُ أَهْلِ الِاخْتِصَاصِ) (gardens of the people of specialty)." The *nasut al-khass* is made from the *ahl al-ikhtisas* (people of specialty) — that is, from the *riyadat* (رِيَاضَات) (spiritual gardens) of the *hadarat* (presences) and the *hudud* (ranks) of the dawah, the term used for them. *Wa jaala al-nasut al-dani min riyadat man dunahum min al-mawani* (And He made the lowly human form from the gardens of those below them in station). The lowly *nasut*, the *tabii*, is made from those of lesser station. The questioner's view — that the *hujaj al-jazair* are the source of the *nasut al-tabii* — is thereby corrected as a mistaken conception, and its reality is to be understood from that first preface. *Tataddahu laka maani* (its meanings will become clear to you). *Wa sun dhalika yasunka Allah fa-huwa min al-sirr al-rabbani* (And guard that, may Allah guard you, for it is from the Lordly secret). These matters — concerning the veils and the *nasut*, how they originated and from what they are made — constitute a great *sirr al-rabbani* (سِرٌّ الرَّبَّانِيّ) (Lordly secret).

*Wa amma qawluka inna al-nasut al-tabii al-zahir min hujaj al-jazair fa-huwa minka ghalat* (As for your saying that the manifest natural human form is from the Hujaj of the Islands, that is a mistake on your part). The view that the *nasut al-tabii*, the most manifest one, is made from the *hujaj al-jazair* is wrong. *Wa man itaqada dhalika fa-qad taradda wa saqat* (And whoever believes that has been rejected and has fallen). *Bal al-awwal fi dhalika ayyuha al-sail innahu yajtamiu fi kulli nasutin riyadatu jami (جَامِع) al-sairin ila al-lahut min 'alin wa danin* (Rather, the first principle in that, O questioner, is that in every human form gather the spiritual gardens of all the travelers to the Divine Realm, from the high and the low). In every *nasut* there gather the *riyadat* of all who travel *ila al-lahut* (to the Divine Realm) — all believers, whether of high station or low.

*Hasbama amma 'anhu sayyiduna al-dai sarimuddin ba'd kutubihi al-aliyah wa ghayruhu min mawalinna al-duat ala al-lawh al-sharif al-altaf fi al-majam' al-nuraniyyah* (according to what our master the Dai Sarimuddin implied in some of his high books, and others from our masters the duat, upon the noble and subtle tablet, in the luminous collections). Sarimuddin here is Sayyidna Ibrahim ibn Muhammad ibn Fahd (سَيِّدُنَا إِبْرَاهِيمُ بْنُ مُحَمَّدٍ بْنِ فَهْد); other *duat* have also pointed to this matter. The *riyadat* ascend within the *nasut*, and the *surat* (forms) ascend within the *lahut*. The *nasut* of the Imam — within which the *riyadat* ascend — and the *lahut* of the Imam — within which the *surat* ascend — stand in perfect correspondence. The *riyadat* that go to a given place in the Imam's *nasut* arrive at precisely that same place in the *lahut*, not at some other — not to the heart or a lower station when they are destined for the ear. Wherever the *riyadat* go in the *nasut*, there the *surat* go in the *lahut*.

*Murad al-hijab al-khass huwa al-mubtana fi al-bayt al-haram* (The intended meaning of the special veil is that which is built in the Sacred House). The *nasut-i khass* (نَاسُوتٌ خَاصّ) has its foundation in the *Bayt al-Haram* (Sacred House of God). *Huwa alladhi la tazibuhu qaraib al-aslab wa al-arham* (it is that which the proximities of loins and wombs do not confine). This *nasut-i khass* is not bound by loins and wombs; it is not born of a mother and father. Its construction takes place in the Sacred House of God. The *tabii*, by contrast, is born of a mother and father. *Wa-la tahwih afniyah wa-la dur* (and neither courtyards nor houses contain it). No courtyard, no ground, no house can contain the *nasut-i khass*; it is so exalted that it lies beyond all of these. *Wa-la yaliju fi 'idam al-qubur* (And it does not enter into the bones of the graves). At the time of death the *nasut-i khass* does not descend into the grave — that is the Imam's affair. *Wa-lladhi yaqau alayhi al-mawt wa al-qatl wa al-fawt huwa adna min al-hujub* (And the one upon whom death, killing, and passing away occur, it is the lowest of the veils). *Li-yaqdiya ma alayhi wa yajib* (so that it may fulfill what is upon it and is obligatory). The states that befall fall upon this lowest veil, not upon the *nasut-i khass*. So it was with Imam Husayn: the killing and all that took place befell the veils; nothing befell the real self. The real self is protected.

As for which specific *hijab* was in use at any given time, no mention of it is found anywhere. Hadrat Gyani Sahib muqaddas (حَضْرَت گِیَانِی صَاحِب مُقَدَّس) would ask whether anyone had read anywhere which veil came into use and when — and it has not been found in any source. Concerning Rasulullah, salawatullah 'alayh, however, it is known that the veil used for his *ghusl* (غُسْل) (ritual bath) was other than the one that was buried. The *ghusl* veil was Abdullah ibn Masud, and the one buried was Abdullah ibn Rawahah. The one present for *ziyarat* (visitation) is Ubayy ibn Kab (أُبَيّ بِن كَعْب). These are the *hijabs* (حُجُب) that are buried. Ubayy ibn Kab holds a great station; he is also among the teachers of Rasulullah. As it is said, Rasulullah himself said that Ubayy is his spiritual mother — *ummi al-ruhaniyyah* — his first teacher, who taught him all the laws and principles of Adam, alayhis salam. After that teaching, Rasulullah became *mamnun* (indebted). In the same way, five teachers taught him, and after their teaching they became *mamnun*. The final teacher was Janab-i Khadijah, before the *bathah* (بَطْحَاء). Janab-i Khadijah's position is that she became *mamnun* to Rasulullah, and she was teacher not only of Sayyidna Rasulullah but of Janab-i Ali as well. For this reason it is said: *Ibn Maryam* (son of Mary) — *Isa Ibn Maryam* (Jesus son of Mary) is Janab-i Ali, and Maryam is Janab-i Khadijah. For this very reason Janab-i Maryam was exempted from the five prayers. The five prayers were exempted because, after the five prayers, Janab-i Ali joined, on account of her being his teacher.

## 8. The Fifteen Ranks of the Repentant and How Far We Fell

Whether Ubayy ibn Kab and all those who stood as *hijabs* knew that they occupied that role is worth examining. The answer is that they did — entirely and without doubt. *Marifah* (gnosis) is indispensable to such a station; without it, such a thing cannot be. These were people of great station. Were they to manifest, they themselves would manifest as prophets and messengers — such is the height of their rank. Yet no incident shows the Prophet teaching them in any outward manner. What passed between them was given from heart to heart.

Across all thirteen years in Mecca, the disbelievers and polytheists dominated with overwhelming force, and across the ten years in Medina most of the time was consumed by battle. Even so, the ethical teachings were given. In Medina, after the Fajr prayer, the Prophet, (ع), would remain seated and ask, "Did any of you see a dream last night?" Someone would reply that they had, and he would announce himself a better interpreter than Yusuf, (ع) — that he was the interpreter — and then give his lesson. In that gathering sat hypocrites, Muslims, and towering figures such as Salman al-Farsi. Whatever the Prophet said, each person drew knowledge from it at the level suited to him. The Prophet would speak, and the subtleties and deeper realities within his words reached whomever they were meant for. This was the beauty of the Prophet's discourse: whoever sought, received.

Several incidents are narrated concerning Sayyidna Ubayy ibn Kab. On one occasion Rasulullah asked him, "O Ubayy, do you know which verse of the Quran is the finest? You are the finest reciter (قَارِئٌ) — you recite abundantly and well. So which verse is the most excellent?" He replied, "Allah and His Messenger know best." The Prophet said, "It is Ayat al-Kursi." That was what Rasulullah said.

On another occasion, Rasulullah was present while Ubayy ibn Kab was praying. Rasulullah was likely seated in the mihrab while Ubayy was engaged in a supererogatory prayer. He called out, "Ya Ubayy!" Janab-i Ubayy heard the voice but continued his prayer. The call came a second time, then a third. Ubayy then quickly completed his salutations and came forward. The Prophet said, "Ya Ubayy, I called you, and you did not come." He submitted, "Ya Rasulullah, I was praying." The Prophet replied, "Who taught you this prayer? Is prayer greater, or my command and call? Is my calling not greater?" A verse then came down in the Quran: that when Allah and His Messenger call, the answer is to be given to the Messenger. The verse is most remarkable, for it does not say that the answer is to be given to Allah and His Messenger; it says that Allah and the Messenger call, and the Messenger is to be answered.
