The treatise continues: *wa taazzaz al-maqam al-sani bi-ghayrihi fi lubnani wa-tashahad fi alf surah li-al-anam* — "And the second station was magnified by other than it in its very essence, and he was witnessed in a thousand forms by mankind." This Imam is one who can manifest in a thousand forms. *wa fi dhalika wujuh wa asbab (أَسْبَاب) wa li-hi al-hujjat al-ujab* — "And in that are aspects and reasons, and for him is the wondrous proof." The aspects and reasons behind his manifestation are many, and his affairs are strange and wondrous.

*wa amma qawluka kayfa kana qabla wujud al-samawat wa al-ardina* — "As for your saying: how was it before the existence of the heavens and the earths." The question concerned what existed before the heavens and the earth. *kanat min al-dhawat al-nadimah* — "it was from the penitent essences." They are the *dhawat al-nadimah* (ذَوَاتُ النَّادِمَةِ) (penitent essences), and from these it came into existence. *allati usila minha ma hasula wa tarattaba khalq al-aflak allatihiya aqsam al-nadimin* — "from which was derived what was obtained, and the creation of the celestial spheres was arranged, which are the categories of the penitent ones." The categories of the *nadimin* (نَادِمِين) were divided into fifteen types. From the first type the celestial spheres (أَفْلَاك) were made, and from these the heavens and the earth were formed.

*wa amma qawluka min ayyi fudulatin kana wujud al-awwal al-jussa fi safil al-ard* — "As for your saying: from what remnants was the existence of the first particle in the lower part of the earth." The question was from which remnants the existence of the *jussa ibdaiyyah* (جُثَّة إِبْدَاعِيَّة) in the earth came about, given that the earth is wide and vast. *fa-talam hadaka Allah li al-sawab wa bawwaaka fi jami al-asbab* — "So know, may Allah guide you to what is right and settle you in all the means" — that is, may Allah guide toward correctness and establish the station in all the *asbab* (means), meaning the *hudud* (ranks). *innama kana wujud al-fudulat al-sharifah illa ba'd dhuhur al-jussa al-ibdaiyyah* — "The existence of the noble remnants was only after the appearance of the originated particle." The *fudulat sharifah* (noble remnants) came into existence only after the appearance of the *jussa ibdaiyyah*. *illa ba'd dhuhur al-jussa al-ibdaiyyah al-dhalifah* — "only after the appearance of the weak originated particle." *wa innama yuqal fudlah (فُضْلَة) illa al-baqiyyah min al-jism bada sudur (صُدُور) al-ruh wa al-nafs* — "And it is only called a remnant, that which remains of the body after the emanation of the spirit and the soul." A *fudlah* (remnant), then, is what remains of the body after the departure of the *ruh* (رُوح) (spirit) and the *nafs* (soul). *bi al-wadih min al-khitab* — "with the clarity of the discourse."

*wa amma wujud al-awwal al-jussa al-ibdaiyyah allati khalafat al-nafs al-kulliyyah* — "As for the existence of the first originated particle which succeeded the Universal Soul." The *jussa ibdaiyyah* is that which has taken the vicegerency of the *Nafs al-Kulliyyah* (النَّفْسُ الْكُلِّيَّةُ). The *Nafs al-Kulliyyah* means the *'Ashir* (عَاشِرٌ) (Tenth Intellect). The *jussa ibdaiyyah* takes a partial vicegerency of the *Ashir* (الْعَاشِر), while the full vicegerency of the *kulli* belongs to the *akhir mawr* — the last person of the *mawr* (مَوْر). *fa-kana min asfa aqsam al-nadimin* — "So it was from the purest of the categories of the penitent ones." The existence of the *jussa ibdaiyyah* arises from the purest categories of the *nadimin* (penitent ones). *allati kana tartibuha ala khamsata ashara darajatan fi al-maalim* — "whose arrangement was upon fifteen degrees in the realms." These *nadimin* have fifteen categories. *wa asfa man habata wa ashrafa man hafaw wa tabata* — "And the purest of those who descended and the noblest of those who stumbled and were trampled." Those who fell are the most pure among them, and those who slipped and were trampled are the most noble.

The *nadimin* too had made a mistake. The *'Ashir* first gave the command: I have acknowledged my mistake, so acknowledge yours and repent. All refused at first, saying *la nasmau wa la nuti'* — "we do not hear and we do not obey." A second command then came to guide them once more. He guided them and said that if they would not obey, he would remove them from that realm. At that point the *musirrin* (مُصِرِّين) (the insistent ones) held to their stubbornness and were removed. The *mutahayyirin* (مُتَحَيِّرين) (the bewildered ones) — those in a state of confusion about what to do — were removed as well. The *nadimin* (penitent ones) repented while falling; they repented even as they fell. Yet within that falling, fifteen categories were formed. The believers stand in the fifteenth category. Of the fifteenth category it is also said that they are in the tenth division, each category having ten divisions. The believers of the *sitr* are thus in the tenth division — the last of the last. From this alone the measure of their station may be estimated.

As for the reason behind the believers' falling, and the depth to which a fall may descend: in the prayer recited on the third night, the *darkat* (دَرَكات) (levels of hell) are mentioned and refuge is sought from them, up to *atiran*, in *dua yazul qadr* (دُعَاء يَزُول الْقَدَر), and *damad* — the garment of *damad* — where it ends, the garment of *damad* being the very last. Within it are the seven *darkat*, and the last of the *darkat*, from which lava comes out.

Those who come later and enter are not within this category. Guidance is received from the Imam or from his *hudud*, and so the lower forms ascend. The lower forms — the forms of the disbelievers and the polytheists — ascend and move forward. So it is with the *'uyafa'* (ضُعَفَاء), as Sayyidna Muhammad bin Tahir (سَيِّدَنَا مُحَمَّدُ بْنُ طَاهِر) has mentioned in *Anwar al-Latifah*: the *'uyafa'* are noble souls who love the Ahl al-Bayt, whether they be non-Muslims, or Muslims who love the Ahl al-Bayt, abide by the shariah, and are mindful of what is permitted and what is forbidden. They love the Ahl al-Bayt and are thus *'uyafa'*, and they gradually ascend and enter the fold of Iman. Whether one is a disbeliever or a non-Muslim, a person who comes and accepts Islam will not be among those fifteen categories.

A question arises about Janab Salman al-Farsi, who was first of another religion — whether he therefore belonged to that category. His reality is different: he was already of the *hudud*. Outwardly he followed another religion and then came here, and so a history, a biography (سِيرَة), was formed; but before all of that, he already was what he was.

The disbelievers do not ascend until they accept Islam. They will not enter directly, because *shirkun zulmun 'azim* — "*shirk* is a great wrong." Allah Taala forgives all sins except *shirk*; He does not forgive *shirk*. This is in the Quran as well. Yet the Quran also says *Innallaha yaghfiru al-dhunuba jamian* — "Indeed, Allah forgives all sins." Allah Taala forgives all sins, but He does not forgive *shirk*, because *shirk* is a great wrong. Allah knows when they will turn back.

For this very reason it is said in Surah Yasin: *dhalika taqdir al-aziz al-alim* — "That is the determination of the Exalted in Might, the Knowing." By *al-aziz al-alim* is meant the *'Ashir* (Tenth Intellect), because the *Ashir* knows how many *mawrs* (مُوَارَات) (cycles) it will take for this troop to be purified, how many *dawrs* (أَدْوَار) (periods) there will be in every *mawr*, and how many Imams will come in every *dawr*. He holds the entire map within his awareness. His station is great and vast. And he was a companion — at one time, a companion of those who fell. The measure of the fall from that exalted station may be estimated from this: the *Ashir* was a soul, an intellect, among them. By not obeying, and by reason of the *wahm al-musawat* (وَهْمُ الْمُسَاوَاة) (illusion of equality), they brought this upon themselves.

*Wahm al-musawat* is a great affliction, for its first principle is this: no person can occupy the same station as another. Two people cannot sit at one station. Even where there are two Imams — as with Hasan and Husayn, *Imama haqqin qama aw qaada*, "Two true Imams, whether they stand or sit" — both are Imams, but their ranks are different. Similarly, where there are two *dais* (دُعَاة) — as with Sayyidna Gulam Husayn Sahib and Sayyidna Husayn bin Ahmad (سَيِّدُنَا حُسَيْن بْن أَحْمَد), for at one time there were two *dais*, or even two *madhuns* (مَأْذُونِين) — their ranks will still differ. In one rank no two persons can sit; every person's rank is distinct. This reality was not accepted. The one who was the third intellect and who later became the *'Ashir* did not accept it, and so the *wahm al-musawat* took hold — the notion that he and the *Mumbaith al-Awwal* (اَلْمُنْبَعِث اَلْأَوَّل) (First Emanation) were of equal rank. Whereas the gaze of the *Mumbaith al-Awwal* was upon the *surah ilmiyyah* (صُورَة عِلْمِيَّة) (intellectual form) of the First Intellect, and his gaze was upon the *surah amaliyyah* (صُورَة عَمَلِيَّة) (practical form) of the First Intellect. From this a difference was created: *nazar-i ashraf* (نَظَرِ أَشْرَف) (the nobler gaze) and *nazar-i adwan* (نَظَرِ أَدْوَن) (the lower gaze).

O Allah, send blessings and peace upon the Fatiha to the soul of Sayyidna 'Ali ibn Hibatullah, may his soul be sanctified and his grave be illuminated, and may its blessings and support be multiplied upon us. Accept from us, O Allah, the benefit and the benefiting, for the sake of Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon them. Al-Fatiha. Bismillah.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> *A note for the reader.* Two terms here reward a closer look. When the treatise closes an
> argument with *bi al-wadih min al-khitab*, the word *khitab* comes from the root kh-t-b, which
> centers on addressing others in serious, formal speech; the same root gives *khutbah*, the
> sermon delivered from the pulpit, and *khatb*, a grave matter that calls for such address. So
> "the clarity of the discourse" carries the weight of formal, deliberate address rather than
> casual explanation — fitting for a chapter that dwells on the Prophet's speech reaching each
> listener at his own level. On the ranks of intellect named here, a related teaching preserved in
> this tradition speaks of the Pillar of Light, the Light of the Word, as another name for the
> Flowing and Pervading Knowledge passed from the Creator to the First Intellect, and from the
> First Intellect connected onward — whoever came into contact with that light truly succeeded.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 9. The Sphere That Rises From Where It Stands

The second question arose from what Imam Ahmad bin Yusuf (الْإِمَامُ أَحْمَدُ بْنُ يُوسُف) had taught concerning the *falak al-mustaqeem* (فَلَكُ الْمُسْتَقِيمِ) (the straight sphere), the *muheet* (مُحِيط) (the encompassing one), and the saying `wa anna noor al-shamsi min noor al-azeem` — that the light of the sun derives from the magnificent light. The questioner stated that he had benefited (اسْتِفَادَة) from his *sayyid* (master), his *madadgaar* (helper), and his Imam — the one upon whom he relied, Ahmad bin Yusuf. In that era, Ahmad bin Yusuf must have held one of the *hudood* (حُدُود), the ranks of the religious hierarchy. From him the questioner learned of the *falak-e-mustaqeem*, also called the *falak-e-muheet*, the highest sphere, which is *ghayr-mari* — invisible. Beyond the *falak-e-mustaqeem* the *'alam-e-ruhani* (the spiritual world) begins, and the light of the sun draws from its light — from the light of the *falak-e-mustaqeem* or *falak-e-muheet*.

From the Imam came a further inquiry: `wa ma yaoodu amr al-falak al-mustaqeem` — what will return of the affair of the straight sphere? What will be its condition `'inda irtiqa' al-rubaataat` — at the time of the ascension of the *rubaataat* (رُبَاطَات)? That is: at the close of the *kawr* (كَوْر) (cycle), when all things reach their conclusion and the skies fall, what will be the state of the *falak-e-mustaqeem*? `Wa ayna yaseeru` — where will it proceed? `Wa ayna suooduhu` — what will its ascent be like? `Wa ma alladhi yuwasilu ilaihi al-ayimmah minhu` — what will the *ayimmah* (Imams) attain from it? `Faqad qeela innahu yasadu` — it has been said that it will ascend — `innahu yasadu min mawdiihi` — that it will ascend from its own place: the *falak-e-mustaqeem* or *falak-e-muheet* will rise from exactly where it stands.

The principle in such matters is otherwise: whoever must ascend, whoever must advance, must first come to the earth and then proceed. The *falak-e-mustaqeem* is different — it will ascend from exactly where it stands. Yet this remained a question, and no reliance rested on the question itself; reliance would rest upon the answer to come.

The questioner then closed: `Wa munni 'alayya` — bestow this favor upon me; `wa jud ala mithli man yurhamu` — be generous to one such as me, upon whom mercy is shown; `Warham man laisa lahu rahimun siwak` — have mercy on one who has no merciful one besides You; `Wa la malan illa iyyaka` — and none will treasure or benefit him besides You.

`Al-jawab 'ala dhalik al-sual` — the answer to that question. It was the *dai* (summoner) Sayyidna Hibatullah who provided it.

The question, restated, concerned the *falak-e-mustaqeem*: whether it would depart from its place, what the *ayimmah* would attain from it, and whether the light of the sun truly derives from the light of the *falak-e-mustaqeem*. The questioner had raised all of these points.

The answer begins: `Aqoolu bi 'awnillah al-kabeer al-mutaal` — I say with the help of Allah, the Great, the Most High — `wa minnati wali (وَلِيٌّ) al-asr wa al-zaman` — and by the bestowal of the Master of the Age and Time — `alayhi salawat al-raheem al-rahman` — upon him be the blessings of the Merciful, the Compassionate — `wa taeed-e mawlaya al-alam` — and by the support of my most learned master, Mawlaya al-Alam. By *alam* is meant Mawlana Hibatullah; that is his title. `Wa sirrihi al-baahir al-muazzam` — and by his luminous and magnificent secret — `anna al-falak al-mustaqeem` — that the straight sphere — `wa anna noor al-shams min noorihi al-azeem` — and that the light of the sun is from its magnificent light: the light of the sun derives from the magnificent light of the *falak-e-mustaqeem*. `Fa dhalika ka dhalika` — the matter as conceived is precisely so.

`Wa la budda an yuwasiluha shayun min anwar al-qalb fi jamee al-masalik` — and it is necessary that something of the lights of the heart reach it in all the paths. The light of the sun must receive lights from the lights of the heart. By "heart" is meant the Baytullah (House of Allah) that stands opposite its heavenly counterpart, the *taamur* (تَأْمُور); from that Baytullah the sun receives its lights. This is why the pronoun used here is feminine — *yuwasiluha*, it will receive them. `Fi jamee al-masalik` — at every moment and in every path.

`Wa qawluka ma yakoonu haal falak al-mustaqeem 'inda irtiqa' al-rubaataat` — as for the question of what the state of the straight sphere will be at the time of the ascension of the *rubaataat*: at the end of the *kawr*, when the final matter comes to pass, the *falak-e-mustaqeem* will be as follows. `Wa yasadu min mawdiihi` — it will ascend from its place. The understanding is correct: it will ascend from its own place.

It will pass into the tenth *barzakh* (بَرْزَخ) (intermediate realm), the tenth *'arsh* (عَرْشٌ) (throne) that is made — though this point is not elaborated — and from there it will ascend. `Wa yakoonu aqlan qaiman bil-fil (بِالْفِعْل)` — and it will become an intellect existing in actuality, `qaiman bil-fil`. Until that point it was `qaiman bil-quwwah (بِالْقُوَّة)` — existing in potentiality: helping others, sustaining the lights of the sun, granting the *ayimmah* their assistance — all of this occurring `bil-quwwah` (in potentiality). Its state `bil-fil` (in actuality) will be that it ascends as an intellect.

`Fi jamee al-haalat wa la budda... wa yakoonu lahu ruhun min noorin... wa maqamuhu ruhun... mashkoorah lahu...` — in all states, and it is necessary... and it will have a spirit of light... and its station will be a spirit... acknowledged for it. Its station will be that of a spirit, for which a full explanation would be lengthy. `Ma yuheetu biha illa al-maqaamaat al-nooraniyyah` — none can encompass it except the luminous stations, meaning the words of the *ayimmah*. `Aw man kaana min daina... al-mutasawwir bi suwar (صُوَرٌ) al-imaniyyah... al-muhawwin lil-mushkilat` — or one who was from their *dai*s, envisioned in the forms of faith, who renders religious difficulties accessible. `Wal-ati bil-khawariq al-adat` — and the one who comes with extraordinary feats, one who possesses great and extraordinary states.

`Wa laqad kashafa lana ma la yakshifuhu` — he has unveiled for us what others do not unveil. `Wa tafaddala 'alayna ma la yuhaat` — and he has bestowed upon us what cannot be encompassed. `Wa la yukayyaf` — and cannot be described; its nature cannot be described. `Jazahumullahu anna ma jaza ahl al-duat` — may Allah reward them on our behalf as He has rewarded the people of the *duat* (summoners), meaning Sayyidna Hibatullah. `Wa ammalana min sirrihi wa barakatihi` — may benefit be received from his secret and his blessings. `Innahu waliyu dhalika` — He is the master of that matter and capable of it. `Ala ma hunalika` — over what is there.

`Wa amma rooh al-imam al-noorani` — and as for the spirit of the luminous Imam — `wa shabhuhu al-qudsani... falam anna dhalika min asrarillah allati la tusattaru bil-aqlaam` — and his sacred likeness... know that this is among the secrets of Allah, which cannot be written with pens. `Wa dhalika anna al-maqam 'alayhi al-salat wa al-salam mata haanat nuqlatuhu ila dar al-naeem al-muqeem, tahallala fil-qalb` — and that is because the station, upon him be prayers and peace, whenever the time of its transfer comes to the abode of bliss, the everlasting, dissolves into the heart. When the time of the Imam's (ع) passing arrives and he departs to the everlasting abode of bliss, his soul dissolves into the heart — into the Baytullah — and from there his ascent takes place.

The same principle holds for the *mumin* (believer) at burial. The *mumin*'s ascent also occurs through the Baytullah, and his *fuzala'* (excellent parts) will ascend through it. His *surat* (form) ascends to the *hudood-e-uliyyah* (higher ranks), while his *riyat* (رِيَاة) (subtle essences) enter the *aflak* (spheres), passing into the sun and Jupiter and beyond. His *fuzala* (فُضَالَة) ascend gradually, in a state of prayer, until they reach the *mihrab* (prayer niche) where he used to pray in the mosque — the very station where he had established his place of worship. From there they proceed to the center, which is the center of his *jazirah* (جَزِيرَةٌ) (island). The center of the two *jazirahs* is said to be the tomb of Sayyidna Sulaiman. From there the ascent continues through twenty-eight stations until the *fuzala* enter the Baytullah. Within the Baytullah stand four pillars; ascending through them, the *fuzala* pass to each pillar in turn and finally arrive at the *rukn-e-aswad* (رُكْنُ الْأَسْوَد), from which the ultimate ascent takes place. These *fuzala*, in their ascending state, are called `zuubat (زُوبَات)`. Once they have ascended, they become `zakhair (ذَخَائِر)` (treasures).

The four pillars are the *asatir* (أَسَاطِير), and they are the *atqiyah* (أَتْقِيَاء) (pious ones), present in their physical form.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> *A note for the reader:* Two terms here carry more weight than a translation can show. *Quwwa* —
> the word behind "in potentiality" (*bil-quwwah*) — comes from a root meaning to be strong, and
> names strength or power in the abstract; in technical usage it denotes a faculty, an inner
> capacity or potency by which a thing is *able* to act. So the sphere described as *qaiman
> bil-quwwah* is not idle but latent: possessed of a real capacity that has not yet issued as
> actuality (*bil-fil*). Likewise *imaniyyah*, from the root -m-n, belongs to the vocabulary of
> security and trust — yielding both *amn*, safety, and *amaana*, a trust one is bound to keep —
> so that faith is the act of placing oneself in secure trust, and the *mumin* is the one who has
> done so. A related teaching preserved in this tradition also speaks of the universal soul as a
> divine force, "the origin from which one seeks help."
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 10. Subtle Essences: What Rises From the Dead and What Feeds the Living

When the time of passing comes, dissolution occurs within the *qalb* (قَلْبٌ) (heart). *Thumma sara mawaddan wa qiwaman li quloob al-ulama min al-hudood* — then he becomes substance and strength for the hearts of the learned ones among the *hudood*. After dissolving, he becomes substance and strength for the hearts of the great *hudood* of the *dawah* (mission). By substance is meant that the great *hudood* of the *dawah* receive support in the strength they already hold: the strength of speech and the strength of benefiting others. That strength reaches them through this dissolved essence. So it was with Janab Yusuf, of whom it is said *lawla an raa burhana rabbihi* (had he not seen the proof of his Lord) — had he not seen the proof of the Lord, he would have erred in what occurred with Zuleikha. That proof of the Lord is the *mawadi* (substance) obtained from the Imam: *Alladheena bi hidayatihim 'urifat taat al-mabood* — those through whose guidance the worship of the Worshipped One is made known.

*Fa idha haanat nuqlatuhum* — so when their time of transfer comes. When these *hudood* pass away, *ila 'alam al-anwar... ittasala dhalika al-maddah bil-qalb al-saafi min al-akdar* — to the world of lights, that substance connects with the pure heart, free of impurities. At the time of their passing, *yarjiu ila aslihi* — it returns to its origin. The substance by which their hearts had been strengthened — received from what dissolved in the Imam's *qalb* — is returned at the moment of departure. These were trusts held temporarily, so that in their decisions, in their benefit to others, and in the conduct of their lives, the *hudood* would not err and would remain safe from evil and sin. They became masters of all those states. But the holding was temporary, not permanent. At the time of passing, the substance reconnects and is returned to its origin — to the pure one, free of impurities.

*Wa yakoonu min maooneen nafsu imam al-sharqi wal-gharbi ma madda lahu min al-qalb* — from among those helpers, the soul of the Imam of the East and the West is formed from what is supplied to him from the heart. Through the dissolution that occurred in the Baytullah, the soul of the next Imam takes shape — the Imam of the East and the West. *Ma madda lahu min al-qalb*: what is obtained for him from the heart. *Wa min al-ma alladhi hawlahu* — and from the water that surrounds it. *Wa shayin min al-bataih allati talyihi* — and something from the marshes adjacent to it. All of this is gathered for that Imam whose soul is in the process of origination.

*Wa mimma zukhira lahu mimma inhallah min awwal al-fitrah... bada awwal al-fatrah, bada tamam al-kawr al-mashhoor* — and from what has been stored for him of what dissolved from the beginning of creation, after the first interval, after the completion of the well-known *kawr*. From that, the Imam's soul receives additions. From the very moment the era was created, treasures have been accumulating; from those the Imam's soul is enriched.

*Yajdharu minhu faidah... 'an idrees al-shurafa'... al-muwasilah min daihi... mishkat al-noor* — from it arises a benefit for the noble ones, connected through his *dai*, who is the niche of light.

This meaning has been clarified by Sayyidna al-Dai al-Ajall al-Aleem Hibatullah bin Sayyidna Ibrahim — *Wa qad awdaha hadha al-mana... al-dai... al-ajall al-aleem hibatullah bin sayyidna ibrahim fi risalatihi al-mawsumah bi saqilat al-quloob min al-fikr, wa al-munawwirah lil-basair, wa al-musfiyah lil-suwar* — in his treatise known as The Polisher of Hearts from Thought, The Illuminator of Insights, and The Purifier of Forms.

*Fi jawab sual al-thani 'ashar* — in the answer to the twelfth question. In that book, answering the twelfth question, Sayyidna Hibatullah wrote *bi qawlin aleem al-maani wa al-gharad* — with words of profound meaning and purpose. *Wa ma fihi abyan al-bayan* — what is written there is the clearest of explanations — *wa awdah al-burhan* — and the most evident proof.

*Wa mazkuruhu fi hadhihi al-masalah wajhayn fil-haqeeqah* — its treatment of this matter has two aspects. The matter is explained in two ways. Sayyidna Ali (ع) said: "I will explain to you the state of the Imam's soul — *fi haqeeqah... fi wajhayn* — in reality, in two aspects — *fi muqabalat al-shahadah* — in comparison to the word of the *shahadah*."

*Wa huwa anna al-asrar... allati la yattaliu 'alayha illa al-ahad min ibadihi* — these are among the secrets that none can know except those who have reached the rank of *ahad* (أَحَدٌ) (unique) from among God's servants. *Wa dhalika anna fil-maqam alayhi al-salam... ma yuqabilu qutbayn al-shahadah fil-hukm* — in the station of the Imam, upon him be peace, there is what corresponds to the two poles of the *shahadah* in its ruling. When the Imam (ع) is set alongside the word of the *shahadah*, *li qawli asdaq al-qaileen* — according to the saying of the most truthful of speakers — God Almighty said:

> إِنَّا نَحْنُ نُحْىِ ٱلْمَوْتَىٰ وَنَكْتُبُ مَا قَدَّمُوا۟ وَآثَرَهُمْۚ وَكُلَّ شَىْءٍ أَحْصَيْنَهُ فِىٓ إِمَامٍۢ مُّبِينٍۢ

The verse appears in Surah Yasin. Two things in it merit attention.

The first is the phrase *kullu shayin* (every thing). What is meant by *shay*? A fan, a light, a carpet — these are also *shay*, things, and they too fall under that category. But here, by *shay* is meant the form of the *mumin* (believer). Wherever Allah Taala has used the word *shay*, it refers to the *mumin* and to the form of the noble *mumin* — as in *kullu shayin ahsaynahu fi imamim mubeen*, and in *innallaha 'ala kulli shayin qadeer* (Indeed, Allah has power over all things), and *wa innallaha bi kulli shayin muheet* (and indeed, Allah encompasses all things), and *innallaha bi kulli shayin aleem* (indeed, Allah is knowledgeable of all things). In each of these usages, *shay* refers to the *mumin*, or to the form of the *mumin*.

The second point concerns *ahsayna* (We have enumerated). The significance lies in what was not said: *jamana* (We have gathered) was not said. In gathering, things become jumbled. In *ahsayna*, each form retains its separate nature and its state remains distinct — meaning that despite being gathered, each retains its individuality and can be distinguished from the rest. That is what the Imams did with their forms: through a single form, they made a manifestation. The reason lies here. Had they been *jama* — gathered — they could not have been separated. But they were *ahsa*, enumerated in such a nature that the individuality of each remains in its own place.

A question was raised: in this, the Imam cannot be encompassed except by the Imam — so does that mean the Imam can also do it through his own power? That enclosure, that everything is as it is, except by the power of the Imam — what does that mean?

The answer: *Kullu shayin ahsaynahu fi imamim mubeen*.

It was remarked in reply: this is, of course, the Imam — but the Imam also does not come within its scope.

Everything will come into it.

It was then said: "I will check it and then say."

*Wa dhalika anna al-maqam fih nasut wa lahut* — the station contains both *nasut* (humanity) and *lahut* (divinity). The comparison is drawn with the word of the *shahadah*. In the Imam there are *nasut* and *lahut*. *Nasut* is the collection of *riyat* (subtle essences).

When a *mumin* passes away, the *riyat* that emerge immediately after the passing are the higher *riyat*. The *riyat* that emerge after burial are the lower *riyat*. From the higher *riyat* — those discharged after death but before burial — the *nasut-e-zilli* (shadowy human forms) of the *nawasi* (نَوَاصِي) (successors) are made. From the *riyat* that emerge after burial, which emerge for only three days — after which what remains are vapors — the *nasut-e-tabii* (natural human forms) are made.

A question was raised: the ones that emerge first are more excellent?

Yes, the ones that emerge first are more excellent. There is also a tradition that the one who has passed away should be buried as soon as possible — guidance from the Messenger that the deceased should not be kept long but buried promptly.

These *riyat* correspond to *nasut* and *lahut*, and correspond to the negation and affirmation of the word of the *shahadah*. The *riyat* do not immediately become *nasut*; they must pass through a process. They travel first to the light of the moon, which draws them, then to Jupiter, then from Jupiter to the sun, then back to Jupiter again. Their principal station is the moon. The luminosity seen in the sky on the 13th, 14th, and 15th nights arises from these *riyat* — their congregation becomes very large during those three nights, and that is why that brightness occurs.

For the one who has passed away, the *riyat* that emerge over those three days carry a spiritual notice to the relatives: whatever is to be done must be done within these three days —

- giving charity
- performing good deeds
- hosting meals
- reciting *sadaqallah*
- holding *fatiha khwani* or *beeswa khwani* (بِیسْوَا خَوَانِی)
- arranging for Quran recitation
- giving in charity

After those three days, whatever is done will benefit the noble form and the *fuzala*. The departed will already have left and will not return for a thousand years. Such is the nature of the *riyat*.

The purified *riyat* then descend like drops of dew and settle on certain fruits — uncooked fruits such as apples and similar things. The Imam and the Imam's wife eat those fruits; from that conception takes place, and from that the *Farzand-e-Imam* (Child of the Imam) is born. From the moment of birth, all the nourishment the *Farzand-e-Imam* takes is *riyat*. Though he appears to be drinking milk or receiving ordinary nourishment, in reality he is receiving *riyat*.

One incident occurred in this regard. Mufaddal bin 'Umar, who was the *bab al-abwab* (بَابُ الْأَبْوَاب) (gate of gates) of Imam Jafar al-Sadiq, came into the Imam's presence while the Imam was eating. The Imam said, "Come, join us in the meal." Mufaddal said, "Mawlana, I have just eaten." The Imam replied, "What you ate, and what we are eating: you have not eaten what we are eating. And what you ate, we do not eat." The indication was that what Mufaddal ate was the outward nourishment Allah has created, while what the Imam ate was *riyat* — and in that, Mufaddal was invited to join.

The *mumin* also receives *riyat*. One occasion is during *wudu* (ablution). As soon as he says Bismillah, the breath he draws and the water that touches his body, entering through the pores, carry the *fuzala* and *riyat* that the *mumin* receives. That is why *isbagh al-wudu* (إِسْبَاغُ الْوُضُوء) (performing *wudu* completely) is held in great esteem. The traditions state that certain acts serve as atonements for major sins, and among them is *isbagh al-wudu fil-burudat* — or *sabarat*, as recorded in one place — meaning that *wudu* is performed with piety in extreme cold. *Burudat* does not refer only to cold weather; it also refers to coldness of temperament — the inner reluctance when one's disposition is not inclined toward *wudu*, when there is laziness within. Performing *wudu* completely despite that inner coldness is its inner meaning. In the outward sense, cold weather; in the inner sense, coldness of disposition — in both, performing it fully.

A question was raised: the *riyat* that are received — are they for the common *mumin* also?

Yes.

For the *mumin* also?

Yes.

Whose *riyat* are these?

The *mumin*'s own *riyat* are received by him. The purified *riyat* ascending from the believers come into the *mumin*s share. Those *riyat* are gathered in the moon; from there, they are connected to the one who performs *wudu* and so on.

That is why a tradition states that after *wudu*, whatever water remains on the hand should be shaken off — for from every drop so shaken, an angel is created who will pray for one's forgiveness. It is also said that after *wudu* one should recite *salawat* (blessings) and Surah al-Qadr, *inna anzalna*, and then pray, and that doing so means one's prayer will not be rejected. This too is counted among the blessings of *wudu*.

The second occasion on which the *mumin* receives nourishment from the *riyat* is at the time of *iftar* (breaking fast) and *sahri* (the pre-dawn meal). That is why it is said that *sahri* should be taken as late as possible — not waking at two in the night to eat, but delaying until the last moment. And the fast should be broken promptly: not first praying and then breaking the fast, but hastening to break it. What is received at those moments is of a very high nature, and one should hasten to obtain it.

A question was raised: the *riyat* that are received — do they become part of the body?

Yes, they become part of the body. They ascend. The precise details of how they become part of the body — whether they go to the heart or elsewhere — he had not read. But they enter the body, as water enters through the pores or through respiration. As soon as one says Bismillah, the breath drawn after that carries nourishment from the *riyat*.

A question was raised: are they his own *riyat*?

No — not his own *riyat*; he has not passed away yet. These are the *riyat* of the believers. The purified *riyat* of the believers gathered in the moon are the source from which these things are obtained by him.

Then came the matter of the one visited in Iran. His name was recalled: Avicenna.

Ali ibn Sina was a great *dai*, a very great *hujjat* (proof). With him was a student who was very dear to him. That student used to say to him: "If you were to claim prophethood, people would not deny you. You are so great, and you have mastery over everything. What field of knowledge is there that you do not command? You have mastery over medicine, over everything." He would smile and give no answer.

Once, while traveling with this student through Iran during a time of snowfall, they reached a certain place at the hour of *maghrib* (the evening prayer). From a distance they saw a man breaking through the settled snow to open water, so that he could perform *wudu*. Both of them watched. Then Avicenna said to the student: "You always used to ask me why I do not claim prophethood — that if I were to claim it, people would not deny me. Today I give you the answer. Three hundred years have passed since the passing of the Messenger, may Allah's blessings be upon him, and yet in his *shariah* (sacred law) there is such an effect — upon his very soul — that today this person, without caring for his own life, is breaking the snow and performing *wudu*."

The night before, these two had been staying at a traveler's inn. When they went to sleep, Avicenna became thirsty. He said to the student, "Please give me some water." The water pot was kept in the veranda, and one had to go outside to fetch it. The student said, "Mawlana, it is very cold. You can drink the water in the morning." The next day, Avicenna brought up the scene they had witnessed: "You were saying that I should make such a claim — yet my own state is this: I could not get my dear student to rise and fetch me water. And this person, three hundred years later, following the *shariah* of the Messenger, is breaking the snow and performing *wudu*." That was the state of it.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> *A note for the reader:* The chapter speaks of what "corresponds to the two poles (*qutbayn*) of
> the *shahadah*," and the word repays a moment's attention. The root q-t-b names the fixed pin at
> the center of a millstone — the still point around which the whole stone turns. A *qutb* is
> therefore any pivot or axis, and by extension the central figure upon whom an entire order
> depends; it is in just this sense that the wider tradition calls the imamate the "pole" of
> religion. Read this way, the two poles of the testimony are not two topics placed side by side
> but two axes upon which a whole structure turns — which is why the station of the Imam can be
> set alongside them at all, and why the correspondence is described as holding "in its ruling"
> rather than merely by resemblance.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 11. Three Veils, Four Ranks, Twenty-Eight

*Nasut* and *lahut* stand in relation to the negation and the affirmation within the word of the *shahadah*, just as negation and affirmation belong to Allah Taala, the possessor of might and power — `li 'izzat wal-jabarut` (for the Almighty and the All-Powerful).

`Wa lil-imam 'alayhi al-salam thalathat hujub` (And for the Imam, (ع), there are three veils). The Imam has three veils — three at the foundation, though other veils are also employed. `Mathalu thalathat jawahir (جَوَاهِرُ) al-nizam` (The example of the three jewels of the order). Just as the word of the *shahadah* contains three jewels — *alif* (أَلِفٌ), *lam*, *ha* — so likewise the Imam has three veils. These point toward three forms of *nasut*:

- *nasut-e-tabii* (the natural human form)
- *nasut-e-zilli* (the shadowy human form)
- *nasut-e-khas* (the special human form)

`Wa kullu imam min mawlana husayn jamaa rutub al-arbaah` (And every Imam from our master Husayn has combined the four ranks). Every Imam from Imam Husayn onward has combined all four ranks.

What occurred with Imam Hasan and Imam Husayn requires explanation. Imam Hasan held two ranks, and Imam Husayn held two ranks. In Imam Hasan's time, the two higher ranks — the ranks of Imamate and succession — belonged to Imam Hasan, which is why he was the Imam: whoever holds the higher ranks is the Imam of the time. The two lower ranks were with Imam Husayn, by virtue of which he stood under the authority of Imam Hasan.

This same pattern had governed earlier ages. Among the progeny of Sayyidna Ibrahim, whoever held the two higher ranks was the Imam of that time. Qaydar was there; Mawlana Ismail was there; and the other Imams of those ages — all of them held the two higher ranks. The prophets of their time held the two lower ranks and were entrusted with the mission. Within the progeny of Sayyidna Ibrahim, and specifically within the progeny of Mawlana Ismail, the same arrangement held: Sayyidna Ismail held the two higher ranks, while the prophet of that time, Ishaq, held the two lower ranks. That pattern continued throughout history.

After the passing of Imam Hasan, all four ranks came together with Imam Husayn and were never divided again. All subsequent Imams held all four. Yet the first Imam with whom the four ranks were truly and fully united was Mawlana Ali Zayn al-Abidin. Even with Imam Husayn, the two lower ranks had dominated over the higher ones, and he too had been subject to that dominance. Once the four ranks were united in Mawlana Ali Zayn al-Abidin, his station was elevated above all preceding Imams. That is why he is called the *Adam-e-Aal-e-Aba* — the Adam of the Prophet's family. Just as Adam was the first of his kind, Mawlana Ali Zayn al-Abidin became the Adam of the Ahl al-Bayt (the People of the House).

A question was raised: did the Messenger also hold the four ranks, and were those ranks given as a trust to Mawlana Ali?

The Messenger did hold the four ranks. With Sayyidna Rasulullah, the two higher ranks were likewise gathered with him — but held as a trust (أَمَانَة). After Mawlana Abd al-Muttalib, the four ranks that had been with him were distributed: he gave the two higher ranks to Mawlana Abu Talib, since Sayyidna Abdullah had already passed away. A grandfather may bequeath to a grandson, so those two remaining ranks were kept as a trust with Sayyidna Abu Talib, on behalf of Sayyidna Rasulullah. When the time came for the appointment of Sayyidna Ali as Imam, Sayyidna Abu Talib gave all four ranks to Sayyidna Rasulullah. The two ranks of mission and prophethood had been descending from Sayyidna Abd al-Muttalib and were rightfully Sayyidna Rasul's. The two ranks of Imamate, however, were given to Sayyidna Rasulullah as a trust — meant for Ali. For Ali. And so:

> `man kuntu mawlahu fa hadha 'aliyyun mawlahu`

Of whomsoever I am the master, Ali is his master. Of whomsoever he was the master — meaning the master of these ranks — Ali is his master. That is the meaning.

`Wa mathalu arbaat rutub... al-muqattaat al-arbaah fil-kalimat al-shahadah: La, ilaha, illa, Allah` (And the example of the four ranks is the four sections in the word of the *shahadah*: La, ilaha, illa, Allah).

`Wa istirahat al-maqam fis-sabi min al-ayimmah` (And the resting of the station is in the seventh of the Imams). The seventh Imam is called the station of rest. With him, the accomplishments of the six preceding Imams are gathered, making it a restful station — one where nothing further remains to be added. The seventh becomes the *jami*, the gatherer. `Min al-ayimmah al-qaim... li man sabaqahu min al-ruh` (From the Imams, the *qaim* — one who rises — for those who preceded him of the spirit). For the past Imams, he becomes a station of the spirit. `Mathalu sabat fusul (فُصُول) fil-shahadah` (The example of the seven sections in the *shahadah*). There are seven sections in the word of the *shahadah*: when it is written in the Arabic manner, there are seven places where the pen lifts from the surface of the paper. Those seven liftings of the pen point to the station of the seventh — the *sabi* (سَابِع) — who is the *jami*, and whose rank is very high.

`Wal-imam majma suwar ithna 'ashar jazirah bil-akhir` (And the Imam is the confluence of the forms of the twelve islands in the end). `Mathalu ithna ashar harfan fil-shahadah al-kabirah` (The example of the twelve letters in the great *shahadah*). The Imam is the confluence: the forms of the twelve islands — the twelve *duat* (summoners) — converge with the Imam at the end, just as there are twelve letters in the word of the great *shahadah*.

`Wa jamee dhalika... thamaniah wa 'ishruna... suratan haddan... nizam al-alam al-ala (نِظَامُ الْعَالَمِ الْأَعْلَى) sarmada` (And the total of all of that is twenty-eight forms as a limit… the order of the higher world, eternally). Added together, two and three and four and seven and twelve become twenty-eight, which is the *nizam al-alam al-ala* — the order of the higher world — eternal. That is a discussion unto itself.

A question was raised concerning the *falak-e-muheet*, which ascends without coming to earth: whether it goes and meets the *barzakh-e-ashir*. The answer given was that this has not been mentioned, or has not been read. It was then observed that there is a verse of the Quran:

> `yanfudhuna la tanfudhuna illa bi sultan`

They pass through; they cannot pass through except with an authority.

Whatever is ascending therefore waits in the *barzakh-e-ashir* until the authority comes. It is deduced from that verse that the same applies to the *falak-e-ashir*. It is not stated explicitly in the text, but the deduction leads there.

Bismillah al-Rahman al-Rahim. This reading of the Fatiha is made a gift — a means of connection, lofty, pure, and pleasing — to the soul of Sayyidna Ali ibn Hibatullah, may Allah sanctify his soul, may his grace be upon us, and may Allah increase us in his blessings. O Allah, accept from us this benefit and the benefiting from it, for the sake of what is contained within it, for the sake of Sayyidna Muhammad and his progeny (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ). Al-Fatiha. And for the sake of Sayyidna al-Dai al-Mutlaq, the chief of the two *dawahs* (الدَّعْوَتَيْن), the *zill* (ظِلّ) (shadow) of the *hadd* (حَدّ) of Sindh, the honor of the noble, the pride of the Arabs and non-Arabs, our master Sayyidna Muhammad Burhanuddin, may Allah sanctify his soul and his sacred mausoleum, may Allah elevate his station and prolong his life, and grant the visit to his noble presence, and for the sake of Sayyidna al-Dai al-Mutlaq, Mawlana Mufaddal Saifuddin, may Allah prolong his noble life. Al-Fatiha.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> *A note for the reader.* The tradition preserves a saying about the two brothers whose shared
> ranks this chapter describes: "Hasan and Husayn are two Imams of truth, whether they rise or
> sit, and their father is better than them both" — a formulation that affirms the Imamate of each
> while still ordering them beneath Ali, much as the discussion above distinguishes higher ranks
> from lower without denying either brother his standing. On the "twelve islands," a modern reader
> may find the word puzzling: *jazirah* here is not geography but a region of the summons, and the
> wider tradition uses the term in just this way when it speaks of those who were "sent into the
> islands" and received commands there rather than in the presences directly. The islands are thus
> the outward field of the call, gathered back at the end to the Imam who is their confluence.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 12. The Middle Way and the Heart Beneath the House

The verse forbids two extremes: the hand kept shut, tied to the neck, so that nothing is given, and the hand opened without limit, leaving the giver blamed and regretful, asking, "What have I done?" The image of the hand upon the neck carries that meaning. Neither bound nor opened without measure.

A parallel verse stands in Surah al-Furqan, in the passage concerning the `'Ibad al-Rahman` (the servants of the Most Merciful):

> *Walladhina idha anfaqu lam yusrifu wa lam yaqturu wa kana bayna dhalika qawama*

"And those who, when they spend, are neither extravagant nor miserly, and there is ever a just middle way between them."

The meaning echoes Surah Bani Israil: one is not to become so miserly that the hand never reaches into the pocket, nor so lavish that nothing remains for family or self, leaving blame and regret behind. The `'Ibad al-Rahman` are praised precisely because, when they spend, they neither waste nor withhold. Their conduct is the middle path. *Khayr al-umuri awsatuha* — the best of matters are those that occupy the middle.

A tension seems to arise here. The Prophet and the noble Imams were known to give everything in charity. The well-known incident is recalled of the Prophet returning from a journey having distributed all his provisions along the way, arriving home with nothing. That state belongs to the `Awliya Allah` (Friends of God), to Rasulullah, and to the `masumin` (the infallible ones).

What once occurred with Amir al-Muminin bears on the matter. A wealthy man came to him with his two sons. The elder son spent freely — upon others and upon himself — and lived well. The younger son took no pleasure in life, citing the example of Amir al-Muminin himself, whose clothing was coarse and whose food was spare and harsh. Amir al-Muminin replied that they should not take his example for their own lives, but should rather benefit from the blessings Allah had given them. He affirmed that what the elder son was doing was correct: when Allah grants blessings, those blessings are to be used.

This is an established narration. There are many incidents of Mawlana 'Ali performing manual labor and giving away his earnings along the road, arriving home with nothing. The same was the state of the Prophet. In the incident recalled here, Sayyida Khadijah had already passed away. The Prophet would slaughter sheep often and in great numbers. He left his house with a goat, slaughtered it, and distributed it. When he returned, only one leg remained. Sayyida Aishah said:

> *Ma baqiya illa hadha*

"Nothing is left but this. You have given everything in charity and alms."

He replied in the same register, and his reply was most eloquent:

> *Kullun baqiya illa hadha*

"Everything remains, except this."

What is given away endures; only what is kept is truly lost.

As for 'Aishah and Hafsah and the troubles they caused the Prophet, this reached such a degree that a Quranic verse was revealed concerning them, in Surah al-Tahrim:

> *Laqad saghat qulubuhuma*

"Your hearts have indeed inclined away from the right."

Their hearts had become crooked. Allah declared that He would not accept their repentance — even though Allah is *Tawwab al-Rahim*, the Acceptor of repentance, the Most Merciful. And if they were to come in opposition, then Allah was sufficient, and Jibril was sufficient, and the *Salih al-Muminin* (صَالِحُ الْمُؤْمِنِينَ), the righteous believers — by whom is meant 'Ali. *La tuzara hidayatuhu*.

A second aspect follows. *Wa fi wajhin akhar* — "And in another aspect." In this second reason, *yuniru min ahl al-basair al-suwar* — it illuminates the forms of the people of insight. *Fa-llahu taala alima ma hadhihi sabila min hadha al-sirr al-ghamiz wa-l-mana* — "So Allah the Exalted knows what this path is of this obscure secret and its hidden meaning." And there is the Quranic verse:

> *Wa jaalna al-bayta mathabatan li-l-nasi wa amna*

"And We made the House a place of resort for the people and a place of security."

Two qualities are thus assigned to the Baytullah:

- a place of reward and `thawab` (divine recompense)
- a place of `amn` (security)

*Wa fi al-qalb mithlu ma fi al-shahadah al-muaddiyah li-qailiha ila dar al-saadah* — "And in the heart is the like of what is in the testimony of faith, which leads its speaker to the abode of felicity." In the heart resides that which is in the `shahadah` (testimony of faith) — the very thing that leads the one who says *La ilaha ill-Allah* toward eternal felicity. *Wa dhalika anna al-qalb al-haqiqi al-mutalali bi-l-nur huwa tahta hadha al-bayt al-mamur* — "And that is because the true heart, gleaming with light, lies beneath this Frequented House." The `qalb al-haqiqi` (true heart) lies beneath the outer house that is circumambulated. *Alladhi hadha lahu ka-l-hijab wa huwa hakhadha ka-l-muhtajib* — "For which this outer house is like a veil, and that which lies beneath is like the veiled one." The outer house of the circumambulation is the veil; what lies beneath it is the veiled reality. *Fi jami al-asbab, mithlu al-nafi wa-l-ithbat* — "In all the means, like the negation and the affirmation." Just as the `kalimat al-shahadah (كَلِمَاتُ الشَّهَادَةِ)` (word of testimony) contains negation and affirmation, so too the Bait al-Mamur and the `bayt al-haqiqi` (true heart) exemplify that same pairing. In another passage it is stated that opposite the Bait al-Mamur there is a `qalb`, a Baytullah facing it — the outer house being the veil and the inner reality being the veiled one.

*Al-shahadah allati man qalaha mukhlisan dakhala al-jannah* — "The testimony of faith, which whoever says with sincerity enters Paradise." An occasion during Hajj, during 'Umrah, illustrates a related point: there was no space upstairs in the Masjid al-Haram, so prayer was offered in the lower level. Just as prayer is valid above the level of the Kabah, it is valid below it as well. The place of prayer is not within the Kabah itself, nor in a basement of the Kabah as such, but below its surface level — and this is permissible on either side, above or below.

*Allati uiddat li-lladhina ahsanu al-husna wa ziyadah* (زِيَادَة) — "Which is prepared for those who do good; for them is the best reward and an increase." Such is the Paradise prepared for those who did good deeds and increased in them. As to what constitutes a good deed, Amir al-Muminin says:

> *Al-hasanatu hubbuna wa-l-sayyiatu bughduna*

"The good deed is our love, and the evil deed is hatred of us."

That is, love and hatred of the Ahl al-Bayt (People of the House). The Quranic verse *li-lladhina ahsanu al-husna wa ziyadah* thus points to those who, in this `karrah (كَرَّة)` (cycle of existence), took the `'ahd al-sharif (عَهْدُ الشَّرِيفِ)` (noble covenant) and thereafter increased in their faith. In the next `karrah`, whatever rank they held, they will be granted a rank beyond it. For those who did good is the best reward and an increase. Their ranks will rise from one `karrah` to the next.

## 13. Registers of Rank: Who Stands Where, and the Twenty-Ninth

It is said in another context that when a believer departs this life at whatever rank he has reached, the power of the Creator — *Bari zubanuhu qudratuhu* — is bound to restore him to that same rank in the next *karrah*, by one means or another. This accounts for why some people advance rapidly and leap forward, apparently from far behind: in their previous *karrah* they were already established at that rank, and their journey now proceeds from that rank onward.

An elder once had occasion to grant *fursat* (an appointment) to someone, and one of the *hudad al-dawah* (حُدُودُ الدَّعْوَة) (ranks of the mission) remarked: "Mawlana, we did not think you would give him *fursat*." The reply was: "We do not grant *fursat* on the basis of present merit alone, but on the basis that the recipient may complete his *hasanat* (حَسَنَات) (good deeds) and virtues." Such persons were already established at that rank from a previous *karrah*, and it was therefore incumbent to bring them to that place. Whatever progress follows thereafter is achieved through their own knowledge and action.

> *Wa fi al-qalb thalatha tajwifat, mithlu al-thalathah al-jawahir*

"And in the heart are three cavities, like the three jewels."

A person often does not himself perceive at what rank he is established. It is said that among the ranks surrounding the Dai are many *hudad* (حُدُود) of very high station who are not yet in a condition to be brought from potentiality to actuality.

An illustration is found among the appointed figures. The first grave in Baroda is the small mausoleum of Mulla Karam 'Ali Sahab. In the time of Sayyidna Hibatullah Sahab, to whom these figures were appointed, the affair of Daud ibn Fi had occurred. Sayyidna Sahab wrote a treatise clarifying the rank-list of the *hudad* and produced a formal register. Daud ibn Fi had claimed to be superior in knowledge and rank to the Dai. He later repented, traveled to Najran, was forgiven by Sayyidna Sahab, and died as a believer — yet his rank was held at the level of a believer. Sayyidna Hibatullah bin Ibrahim Sahab compiled his register and placed Sayyidna Mulla Karamullah Sahab — who was the Dai, or *mansub* (مَنْسُوب), of two *jazirah* (islands), namely Hind and Sindh — at the twenty-eighth position. Twenty-seven persons stood above him in that list. The eighth position was held by Sayyidna Ismail bin Hibatullah Sahab. The first position was held by Sayyidna Ali bin Hiba's — the author of the very book being read here — upon whom *nass sharif* (نَصٌّ شَرِيف) (noble designation) had been performed. He, however, passed away. Sayyidna Sahab therefore passed over the seven above Sayyidna Ismail and performed *nass sharif* upon the eighth, Sayyidna Ismail bin Hibatullah Sahab.

Such things occur. In the time of Sayyidna Husayn bin Hasan (حُسَيْنُ بْنُ حَسَن), *qaddasa Allahu ruhahu*, it happened that Sayyidna 'Ali bin Husayn al-Mukarrami, who was the *sahib al-waqt* (صَاحِبُ الْوَقْتِ) (master of the time), was unable to attend the Friday gathering for some reason. The mosque was full. He sent his Abyssinian servant, Rayhan, with the message that Husayn bin Hasan would lead the prayer. There were, however, fifty men named Husayn bin Hasan in attendance, and people asked Rayhan which one was meant. Rayhan returned to Sayyidna Sahab, who replied in a loud voice, reciting the full lineage for ten generations: "Husayn bin Hasan bin Abdullah bin Ahmad al-Mukarrami." Sayyidna Husayn bin Hasan was seated at the eighth or tenth position, with five *manasib* (مَنَاصِب) (high-ranking officials) before him — among them the *mansub* of Yemen and Najran, Sayyidna Muhammad bin Jamali, and the father of Sayyidna Ahmad bin Jamali. All the *manasib* were present. Yet Sayyidna Ali bin Husayn chose Sayyidna Husayn bin Hasan to lead the prayer.

When Sayyidna 'Ali bin Husayn later came and brought Sayyidna Abdullah ibn Hamid with him, he announced to all who were being taught by the *hudad* that he was departing for Hindustan and that Husayn bin Hasan would look after all affairs in his absence. The community understood this to be a *nass sharif*. It also became clear that Sayyidna Abdullah bin Hamid (عَبْدُ اللَّهِ بْنُ حَامِد), who was being taken along, would not become the Dai afterward, for it is a principle of the mission that the Dai of the three *jazirah* cannot leave the *jazirah* of Yemen — the *qalb* (heart) — without leaving his *naib al-maqam* (deputy) there. Since Sayyidna Ali bin Husayn left that station and took Sayyidna Abdullah bin Hamid with him, appointing Sayyidna Husayn bin Hasan in that place, the meaning was plain.

On the matter of what lies beneath the Baytullah: in another passage it is mentioned that beneath the basement of the Baytullah is the *sakhrah* (صَخْرَة) (the sacred rock), which is a distinct and separate matter, not directly in the basement. In the *sakhrah* there are ten *abrar* (righteous ones). The last among these ten is Nimrud, he who claimed divinity, saying *Ana Rabbukum al-Ala* — "I am your Lord, the Most High."

> *Wa fi al-ithna 'ashar al-jazirah, ithna ashar qalban sharifan, mithlu al-ithna ashar harfan fi al-shahadah*

"And in the twelve islands are twelve noble hearts, like the twelve letters in the testimony of faith."

The twelve *jazirah* each correspond to a *qalb* (heart); every *jazirah* is itself a *qalb*. This mirrors the twelve letters of *La ilaha ill-Allah*.

> *Min qawlihi al-jamil, jami dhalika thamaniah wa 'ishruna addan. Li-l-diyanah wa-l-huda. Fa-sun hadha al-sirr al-masun*

"From his beautiful saying, the total of all this is twenty-eight in number, for the religion and the guidance. So preserve this guarded secret."

The number twenty-eight is always maintained. Among the *hudad*, the twenty-eighth position — such as that of Sayyidna Jafar bin Sulayman — is of very high station. Likewise, the twenty-eighth Imam, Sayyidna Maadd, Mawlana Maadd, is of very high station. Yet the twenty-ninth is also of extraordinary station.

What pertains to the Prophet bears this out. The *kalimat al-shahadah* appears in five forms in the Quran:

- *La ilaha ill-Allah*
- *La ilaha illa hu*
- *La ilaha illa ana*
- *La ilaha illa alladhi*
- *La ilaha illa anta*

The last form, *La ilaha illa anta*, is rare, appearing only once in the Quran — in the glorification of Yunus (ع). The rest are repeated. And *Muhammadun Rasulullah* likewise appears only once in the Quran, unrepeated, in Surah al-Fath, in its twenty-ninth verse. In the sequence of messengers he was the twenty-ninth. Janab 'Ali (ع) was the twenty-eighth, and the twenty-eighth holds a very high station. The twenty-ninth, whether among the Imams or among the *duat* (missionaries), is rare; he enters into the sequence, disrupting its ordinary flow. Rasulullah, *sallallahu alayhi wa sallam*, entered the line of Imams — not as an Imam in the ordinary sense, for after Imam Janab Abu Talib it should have been Janab Ali. But he entered, and Ali became his follower, because in him all four ranks were combined: *nubuwwah* (نُبُوَّة) (prophethood), *risalah* (messengership), *imamah* (leadership), and *wasayat* (وَصَايَت) (succession). He thus became the master even of Ali. This was a station established for Janab Rasul in the *alam al-ruhani* (عَالَمُ الرُّوحَانِي) (spiritual world).

The same pattern holds among the *duat*. Sayyidna Jafar bin Sulayman is the twenty-eighth, and the twenty-ninth is Sayyidna Muhammad bin Faraj (مُحَمَّدُ بْنُ فَرَج). Although Sayyidna Muhammad bin Faraj was a *dai ghayr munfarid* (دَاعٍ غَيْرُ مُنْفَرِدٍ) (non-absolute missionary), he became *munfarid* (مُنْفَرِد) (absolute), having been established in the rank of the *munfaridin*. Sayyidna Jafar passed away during his lifetime, yet Sayyidna Muhammad bin Faraj was established in the rank of a *hujjah* (proof) — one who is the equal of thirty *duat*. After Sayyidna Jafar, every Dai who has come has been established in the rank of *hujjah*, without exception, until today. This is the status of the *qaim al-duat* (قَائِمُ الدُّعَاةِ) (established missionaries). When Sayyidna Jafar bin Sulayman was established in the rank of *hujjah*, the *dai al-balagh* (دَاعِي الْبَلَاغ) (missionary of communication) of the three *jazirah* — that rank — became vacant, and *nass sharif* was performed upon it. He is therefore retained in the sequence.

This sequence is found in the old *sahifah* (books) — those of Sayyidna Ghulam Sahab, Sayyidna Farhat Ali Sahab, and Sayyidna Ziyayi Sahab Muqaddas, *Zul Maani* (ذُو الْمَعَانِي) — where no mention of Sayyidna Muhammad bin Faraj appears. When the new *sahifah* was compiled, his mention was likewise omitted, in keeping with the older books. But when Sayyidna 'Ali bin Husayn reviewed the manuscripts, he studied them carefully, declared that everything was correct, and raised two or three points. One of those points concerned Sayyidna Muhammad bin Faraj.

The status of Sayyidna Muhammad bin Faraj is that he is the twenty-ninth. The twenty-ninth, wherever he appears, enters into the sequence — just as Rasulullah entered the sequence as the twenty-ninth. Surah al-Fath embodies the same principle: nowhere in the Quran did Allah Most High mention Rasulullah by name in his capacity as Rasul except in Surah al-Fath, and there in the twenty-ninth verse. The twenty-ninth verse of Surah al-Fath opens with *Muhammadun Rasulullah* — "Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah."

The *muqattaat* (مُقَطَّعَات) (disjoined opening letters of the Quran) are twenty-nine in total. Of the 6,666 verses of the Quran, the letters may be classified in more than one way. One classification distinguishes dotted from undotted letters. Another distinguishes *nurani* (نُورَانِي) (luminous) from *zulmani* (ظُلْمَانِيّ) (tenebrous) letters. The twenty-eight letters of the Arabic alphabet are arranged in a scheme of four groups of seven:

1. the first seven are *nurani khafifah* (نُورَانِيَّةٌ خَفِيفَة) (lightly luminous);
2. the second are *nurani thaqilah* (نُورَانِيَّةٌ ثَقِيلَة) (heavily luminous);
3. the third are *zulmani khafifah* (ظُلْمَانِيَّة خَفِيفَة) (lightly tenebrous);
4. the fourth are *zulmani thaqilah* (ظُلْمَانِيَّة ثَقِيلَة) (heavily tenebrous).

In Surah al-Fatihah, the summary of the entire Quran, one *zulmani khafifah* letter does appear. But there exists one location in the Quran where no *zulmani khafifah* or *zulmani thaqilah* letter appears at all: the *muqattaat*. Not a single *zulmani* letter — whether lightly or heavily tenebrous — occurs in the *muqattaat*. This is why their status is very high, and why the *duat* have used them as a *wasilah* (وَسِيلَة) (means of supplication) in *tawizat* (تَعْوِيذَات) (amulets) and in certain prayers. In the brief daily supplications of Imam Jafar al-Sadiq, the second supplication employs *Kaf-Ha-Ya-Ayn-Sad* as a *wasilah*, and this entire supplication has been translated. The *muqattaat* function as a *wasilah*, and there are twenty-nine of them in all.

Among the *muqattaat*, some are repeated and some are unique. *Alif-Lam-Mim* appears many times, whereas *Nun* appears only once, and *Ya-Sin* only once. From the non-repeated *muqattaat*, one sentence — and only one — can be formed: *Siratu 'Aliyyin haqq, numsikuhu* — "The path of Ali is truth; we hold to it." No other sentence can be formed from them.

In translations of the Quran produced for general audiences, the *muqattaat* are either passed over or accompanied by a note that only Allah knows their meaning — or that, at most, His Prophet knows it. This is because those translators did not remain attached to the Ahl al-Bayt, and so the knowledge did not reach them. Even the Shia, who profess love for the Ahl al-Bayt, express their *walayat* (وَلَايَت) (devotion) fervently, and genuinely love them, do not possess the knowledge held here. The realities of *lahut*, *naut* (نَاوُت), *nasut*, the *nafs-i-imam*, and matters of that nature are entirely absent from their understanding. This reveals that with Imam Jafar al-Sadiq two dimensions were always present: an outward following, whose adherents were his Shia in the common sense, and a *batini* (inner) following, which was separate and distinct. The *batini* Shia moved forward gradually, steadfast in their faith, firm in their silence and constancy.

Bismillah al-Rahman al-Rahim. Al-hamdu lillahi rabb al-alameen. Wa-l-salatu wa-l-salamu 'ala sayyid al-mursalin. Wa ala alihi al-tayyibin al-tahirin. Wa ala hudaat dinihi al-mayamin. Wa-l-anatu ala adaihim ajmain ila yawm al-din. Amin. Rabbana taqabbal minna innaka anta al-sami' al-alim. Wa tub 'alayna innaka anta al-tawwab al-rahim. Sayyidna wa Mawlana al-Mansur al-Mutlaq fi al-Dunya wa al-Din, Sayyidna wa Sharaf al-Dalil, al-Alim al-Azim wa al-Haj al-Sharif, Sayyidna Ghulam Husayn Sayyidna al-Mansur al-Muqaddas, wa walid Nasrullah Abdullah Hibatullah bin al-Mansur al-Muqaddas, *qaddasa Allahu ruhuma*. Wa *tawwala Allahu baqa* Mawlana azz taala, yaquruhu wa yahmihi wa yuidduhu wa yukfihi wa yushfihi, min sharri jami' al-amrad wa-l-asqam wa-l-awja wa-l-alam, min sharri al-ghilal wa-l-anzam, wa min sharri al-nuhusat wa-l-khusufat wa-l-taslisat wa-l-tarbiat wa-l-takhayil wa-l-mawazir, wa an yudim nasrahu wa yahfazahu wa yakhtam 'umrahu wa yublighahu maratib al-saniyah wa yahmihi fi al-hidayat al-ghurrah wa-l-shakhsiyyah, wa mattaana bi-tuli hayatihi wa baqqa li-maqamih wa darajatihi, wa ahshurna iyyakum fi saat al-dawah wa bi-jawari hudud al-qudsuna, bi-jahi Sayyid al-Mursalin, Sayyidna wa Mawlana al-Imam al-Mubin, wa Ahl Baytihi al-Tayyibin al-Tahirin, salawatullahi wa salamuhu alayhim ajmain. Al-Fatihah. Bismillah al-Rahman al-Rahim. Al-hamdu lillahi rabb al-alameen. Al-Rahman al-Rahim. Maliki yawm al-din. Iyyaka nabudu wa iyyaka nastain. Ihdina al-sirat al-mustaqim. Sirat alladhina anamta alayhim ghayril maghdubi alayhim wa la al-dallin. Amin.

## 14. The Black Stone and the Cycles of Return

In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful. In Him is all help sought, and may Allah's blessings and peace be upon His Messenger, our Master Muhammad, and the family of Muhammad.

These two are the counterparts of the Kalima-e-Shahadat already mentioned. Sayyidna Ali bin Hibatullah (سَيِّدُنَا عَلِيّ بِن هِبَةِ اللَّه) said:

"fa-sun hadha al-sirr al-masun, yasunaka Allah, wa-hfaz ma ulqiya ilayka, yahfazaka Allah, wa hadha min al-asrar al-izam allati la yakad yastur bil-aqlam, wa dhalika mimma ajrahu."

"Guard this preserved secret, and Allah will guard you. Preserve what has been cast upon you, and Allah will preserve you. This is among the great secrets that pens can scarcely set down, and it is from what He has caused to flow."

These are matters of the most magnificent secrets — of a nature that cannot be committed to writing. Whatever has been written here flowed upon the tongue by the grace of the Mawla. Sayyidna Hibatullah said of them:

"wa lam yakun qabla dhalika tara fi qalbi wa janani, wa hiya nuqtah min bahri 'ulumihi wa hibatihi, wa tuhfatun min tuhafihi, wa sadaqa min sadaqatihi, wa qad saqatha ayyuha al-... ilayka hadiyah mubaradah bi-ghayri taabin alayka, wa mathaluha la yahsul illa bil-ibtila wal-hijr wal-hijrat wal-firaq lil-ahl wal-awtan, fa-ahmad mawlaka fi ghuduwwika wa masaka."

"Before this, these matters had not arisen in my heart or my soul. It is a single drop from the ocean of his knowledges and his gifts, a gift from among his gifts, a charity from his charities. These things have reached you, O brother, as a cooling gift that brings peace, without any toil on your part. Their like is not attained except through trial, through separation and emigrations, through leaving one's family, one's cities, and one's homelands. So praise your Master in your morning and in your evening."

No effort was made, and yet this came. Seated in one place, what others obtain only through exile was obtained.

Then the third question: "qad 'alimtu mimma jawwaba Zain al-Abidin ala Umm al-Nada (أُمُّ النَّدَى) min qabli... umr al-dunya" — "I have already taken note of what Mawlana Zain al-Abidin answered to Umm al-Nada concerning the age of the world." Umm al-Nada was a woman of great standing, among the Hudud al-Dawat and a woman of deep learning. She came to Imam Zain al-Abidin (ع) and said, "Mawlana, tell me how much time has passed over the world, and how much remains." He replied, "I will tell you how much has passed, but I will not tell you how much remains. You do not have the istihqaq (merit) for that." He then told her that one mawr had passed, and that they were living within the kawr — the dawr — of the second mawr.

The questioner continued: "wa qad qala Sayyidna al-Qadi Sarim al-Din qaddas Allahu nafsahu wa razaqna shafaatahu bi-ridaka 'anna, anna al-rukn min fudulat (فُضُولَات) Amir al-Muminin al-qadimah fi al-akwar al-ula" — that Sayyidna al-Qadi Sarim al-Din, may Allah sanctify his soul and grant the provision of his intercession and be pleased with them, has said that the Rukn is from the ancient remnants of the Commander of the Faithful in the first cycles.

Sarim al-Din is Sayyidna Ibrahim bin Muhammad Faad, the questioner's grandfather. The Rukn al-Asad — spelled alif, sin, ayn, dal — is the most blessed corner of the Baytullah, the corner in which the Hajar al-Aswad is set. This Rukn al-Asad, it is said, is composed of the fudulat (remnants) of Amir al-Muminin from the first akwar (cycles). This was set down "fi waraqatayni bi-khatti Sayyidi Safi al-Din Ahmad bin Ismail" — "in two pages in the hand of Safi al-Din Ahmad bin Ismail," who was among the Hudud of that era — "fi awwal al-jussah al-mudhamam," in the first great corporeal form. In those first akwar, Amir al-Muminin underwent the rajat (return) repeatedly, born and then passing away, and through successive cycles their accumulated fudulat formed the Rukn al-Asad.

From this arose further questions: "wa fi ayyi kawr zahara amruhu, wa kayfa kana zuhuruhu, wa hal yaqiu 'alayhi al-subhaniyyah... wa kam min hadha umr al-dunya" — "In which cycle did his affair appear, and in what manner did it manifest? And does the term Subhaniyyah apply to him? And how much of the age of the world has this encompassed?" The specific question about Subhaniyyah is this: when one says "Subhan Allah," is it permissible to apply the word "Subhan" to Amir al-Muminin as well?

A questioner raised a difficulty concerning the fudulat of Amir al-Muminin: the Aimmah are all riyat, and from the perspective of the purity of their din, fudulat are not generated in their case at all.

The answer given was that the question is a good one. What fudulat means here is not what fudulah means in the case of an ordinary Mumin. In the case of the Aimmah, all such matter becomes riyat and departs; within three days, what remains is confined to the grave, and the rest becomes flowing riyat. What fudulat means here is their remaining parts. As was explained in a previous lesson, their riyat, which are stored, are housed in the Baytullah and divided into five portions. One portion goes to the Hudud al-Dawat, through which they arrive at correct decisions, are preserved from evils, and their standard in taqwa (تَقْوَى) (piety) is elevated. That one-fifth portion of their riyat is also called fudulat in one sense, and it is returned to the Baytullah when the Imam passes away. So fudulat here means their riyat in a progressed state, and those progressed riyat come to the Baytullah.

The questioner objected that this has taken a material form — that the Rukn al-Asad has been formed, that a stone has been formed. The answer was that it is not material in that sense. The nature of the Rukn al-Asad itself is lofty; what is being pointed to are the blessings within the stone.

The questioner then said that the teaching regarding the blessed stones — 'aqiq (عَقِيق) and yaqut (ruby), which are the fudulat of the Duat Kiram — is readily understandable, but that the fudulat of Mawla Ali and the Aimmah Kiram are all riyat. The answer was that fudulat in the ordinary sense are not generated in their case. In one sense alone are they called fudulat: the remaining matter left from their bodies. Sometimes riyat are also called fudulat. Those fudulat progress through the moon and ascend to Jupiter; one part of them descends and forms the body of the coming Imam, and some part comes to the Baytullah, where one-fifth is received by the Hudud al-Dawat. It also occurs that the fudulat of Amir al-Muminin — which have progressed and exist in a purified state — come to the Rukn al-Asad of the Baytullah. The word fudulat in this passage raised the same question when it was first read; what is meant by it here is riyat.

The question closed:

"wa qad tabaddala al-mashriq maghriban wal-shimal junuban. tafaddalu bil-jawab al-shafi. tawwala Allahu subhanahu bi-amarikum."

"And the east has become the west, and the north has become the south. Do us the favor of the sufficient answer. May Allah, glory be to Him, lengthen your lives."

The answer: "al-jawab 'ala dhalika al-sual, naqulu bi-awn Allah al-Kabir al-Mutaal wa minnat Wali al-Asr wal-Zaman (وَلِيُّ الْعَصْرِ وَالزَّمَانِ), alayhi salawat al-Rahim al-Rahman" — "The answer to that question: we say, with the help of Allah, the Great, the Most High, and with the grace of the Master of the Age and the Time, upon whom be the blessings of the Merciful, the Compassionate" — and with the support of Mawla al-Alam and his brilliant and magnificent secret, meaning the support of Hibatullah Sahab. "awrada Sayyidna Sarim al-Din... bi-anna al-Hajar al-Aswad min kathif Amir al-Muminin al-muwajjad" — "Sayyidna Sarim al-Din has presented the matter thus: that the Hajar al-Aswad is from the kathif of Amir al-Muminin al-muwajjad," may Allah sanctify him in Illiyyin.

By kathif (كَثِيف) is meant those riyat which are purified and which find a place in various noble stations. One such noble station is that they become the very body of the Imam — the body of the Imam who is to come. Throughout his life, the food consumed by the present Imam, and the food consumed by the future child, is all riyat. And because the Muminin are connected to those Imams and profess their walayat (allegiance), they receive a share of that sustenance.

So it is in Surah al-Furqan, in the verses concerning 'ibad al-Rahman (servants of the Merciful):

"wa-jalna lil-muttaqin imama"

"And make us an Imam for the righteous."

A man was once circumambulating the Baytullah reciting this verse, for it appears among the verses of tawaf (طَوَاف). Imam Jafar al-Sadiq, or Mawlana Ali Zain al-Abidin, said to that man: "You have asked Allah for something far beyond your station. You cannot become the Imam of the righteous; you do not have that standing." Though it is a Quranic verse, in dua (supplication) one should instead say "wa-jalna min al-muttaqin," meaning: make us from among the righteous.

"fa-dhalika huwa al-haqq alladhi la shakka yatarihi wal-sirr al-khafi alladhi la maryata fihi."

"That is the truth which no doubt can befall, and the hidden secret in which there is no ambiguity."

Then: "wa amma qawluka, wa fi ayyi kawr zahara sallallahu 'alayhi wa alihi al-ghur" — "As for your question about in which kawr he appeared, blessings upon him and his luminous family" — "faqad qala al-Nabi alayhi al-salam, ana ibn al-karrat (أَنَا ابْنُ الْكَرَّاتِ) wa mumtahin al-awqat," the Prophet ﷺ said:

"ana ibn al-karrat wa mumtahin al-awqat."

"I am the son of the recurring cycles and the one tried by the times."

Birth has come across various recurring cycles, and testing across various ages. In one narration he said: "Allah tested me seven times before the risalat (prophethood), and seven times after." Amir al-Muminin likewise says: "Rasulullah tested me seven times before making me his wasi (successor), and seven times after." He recited the full list of all fourteen tests in a single gathering, asking, "Did this not happen?" — and the entire gathering testified: "Yes, you speak the truth; it happened just so." The Awliya Allah (friends of Allah) all pass through such tests.

Testing is intrinsic to the system of the dawat (دَعْوَة) (mission). Until a person is made to pass through a test, he is not established in a rank. Sayyidna Yusuf bin Sulayman, the first Dai in Hindustan and the grandfather of Sayyidna Sulayman Sahab, was a man of great standing and magnificence. The last Dai of the Bani Anf was Sayyidna Dai Muhammad Husayn, after whom no further duat came from that line, and he put Yusuf bin Sulayman through a most severe test.

Yusuf bin Sulayman lived in a distant village and would come early each morning to study with Sayyidna Sahab. One day he arrived and the hajib (حَاجِب) — the guard of Sayyidna Muhammad bin Husayn — told him to go back, saying the Sayyidna did not wish to receive him. For forty days he came continuously at his usual hour, and each time he was turned away. On the fortieth day the hajib said, "Go away." But at that very moment the Dai called out from his veranda: "Let Yusuf come to me." He came, and the Dai embraced him. That was the test. In this way, through various such trials, he was made ready.

Sayyidna Husayn bin Ismail (سَيِّدُنَا حُسَيْنُ بْنُ إِسْمَاعِيلَ) once remarked that there were two periods in the dawat when the Hindustani muhajirin (emigrants) outnumbered the muhajirin of Yemen and Najran: the time of Sayyidna Yusuf bin Sulayman, and the time of Sayyidna Hibatullah Sahab.

"wa qad suila Mawlana al-Sadiq haythu qala, kunna fil-adwar al-madiyyah wal-huqub al-khaliyah."

"Mawlana al-Sadiq was asked, and he said: We were in the past cycles and the bygone eras."

In the cycles that have passed, there was presence. Of Aristotle he said:

"Kana Aba Muhammad Aristatalis minna Ahl al-Bayt."

"Aba Muhammad Aristotle was from us, the People of the House."

Imam Jafar al-Sadiq even mentioned his kunyah (patronymic): Aristatalis, whose title was Aba Muhammad, was from the Ahl al-Bayt. Of Salman al-Farsi he likewise said:

"Salman minna Ahl al-Bayt, yusalla wa yusallam 'alayhi."

"Salman is from us, the People of the House; blessings and peace are to be sent upon him."

The blessings sent upon them are sent upon him. The station of Janab Salman is therefore very lofty. He is the only person in the entire dawat, throughout the entire Dawr al-Sitr, who became the Bab al-Abwab (Gate of Gates) for both the Nabi and the Wasi. He became the Bab al-Abwab for the Prophet, the Master of the Universe. Janab Khadijah had also become a Bab al-Abwab; Salman al-Farsi (ع) held that rank as well, and he likewise became the Bab al-Abwab of Janab Ali (ع).

"fa-qala lahu al-sail, ya Mawlai, kuntum bi-ashkhasiukum am bi-ayanikum. fa-qala lahu bal bi-ashkhasiina wa ayanina."
